### `fourth-abrahamic-eschatology-precedent`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- laird-climate-narrative-2022 | apocalyptic environmentalism, political will, and the no-choice narrative
- tope-gawd-climate-2012 | the author's earlier formulation of the religious analogy
-->

**Short:** Climate advocacy sometimes places sound ecological concern inside an apocalyptic political story: catastrophe is imminent, one policy direction becomes the only responsible response, and disagreement with that response is treated as indifference to the planet.

**Deployments:** Chapter 4 §4.1 — the climate-apocalypse paragraph.

Frank N. Laird examines the recurring *“Save the Earth”* narrative in climate advocacy. He identifies three linked claims: the earth must be saved, there is no real choice about the response, and political will is the missing ingredient. He places this pattern within what other scholars call *apocalyptic environmentalism*. When the stakes are presented as planetary survival, doubt and compromise become morally suspect, while the proposed remedy appears to be available already and obstructed chiefly by political resistance.

Parag Tope used the religious analogy earlier in “A Fart Tax and a Pink Revolution Can ‘Save the World’” (2012), which called this formation a doomsday cult and coined ***GaWD*** (*Global Warming Deity*). That article establishes the author's prior use of the argument. Laird independently documents the apocalyptic, no-choice, and political-will structure on which the present chapter builds.

Sources: Frank N. Laird, [“The ‘Save the Earth!’ Narrative Creates a Narrative Trap for Climate Advocates”](https://doi.org/10.3389/fclim.2022.900672), *Frontiers in Climate* 4 (2022), article 900672; Parag Tope, [“A Fart Tax and a Pink Revolution Can ‘Save the World’”](https://quicktake.wordpress.com/2012/12/06/indias-pink-revolution-can-save-the-world/), *Quick Take*, December 6, 2012.

### `brahmi-devanagari-structural-identity`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- unicode-standard-chapter-14-brahmi | Brāhmī structure, signs, and relation to later Indic scripts
- unicode-standard-chapter-12-indic | regional change, writing materials, and shared encoding features
- unicode-indic-faq-rendering | font- and display-engine-dependent glyph forms
-->

**Short:** ब्राह्मी (*Brāhmī*) and देवनागरी (*Devanāgarī*) are different visible implementations of the same broad Indic encoding architecture. The scripts changed as they moved across regions, languages, writing materials, and technologies. Modern digital systems can even display one encoded sequence through different glyph forms. These changes affect the audiograph, not the prior sonomeric architecture that it renders.

**Deployments:** Chapter 13 §13.3 and Appendix Part 3 §§3.4 and 3.6 — support the distinction between the prior sonomeric architecture and the scripts that render it, the effect of changing media and technologies on visible forms, and the separate question of possible glyph transmission.

ब्राह्मी (*Brāhmī*) and देवनागरी (*Devanāgarī*) belong to one historical script family, but they are not identical. Their shared encoding principle includes:

- **Abugida structure.** In the general Sanskrit-facing Brāhmī system, each consonant glyph contains an inherent /a/; other vowels attach as positional modifications of the consonant glyph rather than as independent letters. Vowels written initially have standalone glyphs. Some regional adaptations differ: Tamil-Brāhmī, for example, developed a mode in which an unmarked consonant could represent the consonant without an inherent vowel.
- **Sanskrit sound distinctions.** ब्राह्मी (*Brāhmī*) provides signs for the stop distinctions carried by the Sanskrit वर्णमाला (*varṇamālā*), independent vowels and dependent vowel signs, and signs including अनुस्वार (*anusvāra*) and विसर्ग (*visarga*). देवनागरी (*Devanāgarī*) inherits and extends this historical system.
- **The स्थान (*sthāna*) and प्रयत्न (*prayatna*) inventory.** When the scripts are taught through the Sanskrit वर्णमाला (*varṇamālā*), their consonant signs follow the language's order by place and effort. The organizing principle belongs to the sound inventory rather than to the visual shapes themselves.
- **Conjunct formation.** Consonant clusters form ligatures combining the constituent consonants, with the inherent /a/ suppressed on the non-final members.
- **Writing direction.** Left-to-right, top-to-bottom.

The two scripts differ at the surface level — the level addressed by the pyramid's glyph-shape comparisons:

- **Glyph shapes.** Brāhmī's letters and Devanāgarī's letters look different. Brāhmī's shapes are simpler and more angular; Devanāgarī's are more elaborate and curved.
- **The headline, शिरोरेखा (*śirorekhā*).** The characteristic horizontal line of देवनागरी (*Devanāgarī*) running across the tops of letters is not present in ब्राह्मी (*Brāhmī*).
- **Stroke ductus and ligature conventions.** The way strokes are formed and the way conjuncts are assembled differ in detail.
- **Numerical signs.** Brāhmī numerals differ from Devanāgarī numerals.
- **Later additions to Devanāgarī.** Devanāgarī acquired dotted forms such as *ज़, फ़, ख़, ग़, क़, ड़,* and *ढ़* for distinctions used by later languages. These are not part of the core Sanskrit वर्णमाला (*varṇamālā*).

The visual interface also changes with its material and technology. The Unicode Standard notes that Brāhmī's descendants developed distinct regional letterforms and records the proposal that palm-leaf writing encouraged changes in southern scripts. Its Indic-script guidance also shows that one encoded Devanāgarī consonant sequence can appear as a ligature or as a half-form followed by a full consonant. The display engine chooses between those forms according to the glyphs available in the font. The encoded sequence remains the same while its visible audiograph changes.

Glyph resemblances between ब्राह्मी (*Brāhmī*) and Aramaic can support contact, borrowing of particular signs, or use of an earlier graphic template. They cannot by themselves explain the Indic encoding model. Aramaic does not contain the inherent-vowel system, dependent vowel signs, विराम (*virāma*), conjunct formation, or full set of Sanskrit consonantal distinctions carried by ब्राह्मी (*Brāhmī*). The historical question of graphic descent and the architectural question of encoding design therefore require separate answers.

The Unicode Standard documents ब्राह्मी (*Brāhmī*) as an abugida with inherent **a**, independent and dependent vowel signs, a विराम (*virāma*), consonant conjuncts, अनुस्वार (*anusvāra*), विसर्ग (*visarga*), जिह्वामूलीय (*jihvāmūlīya*), and उपध्मानीय (*upadhmānīya*). It also identifies ब्राह्मी (*Brāhmī*) as the historical source of the modern Indian writing systems, including देवनागरी (*Devanāgarī*) and Tamil. These documented features support the shared encoding principle without erasing the historical and regional differences among the scripts.

Sources: Unicode Consortium, [*The Unicode Standard*, Chapter 14, “Brāhmī”](https://www.unicode.org/versions/Unicode16.0.0/core-spec/chapter-14/); [Chapter 12, “South and Central Asia-I”](https://www.unicode.org/versions/Unicode16.0.0/core-spec/chapter-12/); and [“FAQ — Indic Scripts and Languages”](https://www.unicode.org/faq/indic.html).

### `durable-script-archive-selection`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- salomon-indian-epigraphy-1998 | inscription types, media, and epigraphic method
- asi-bharatshri-about-epigraphy | official overview of the Indian epigraphic archive
-->

**Short:** The surviving Brāhmī archive includes royal proclamations, public and donative inscriptions, seals, coins, cave records, graffiti, and marks on pottery. Durable media preserve monumental authority especially well, but they do not preserve only apex speech. The archaeological record dates surviving inscriptions; it does not date the prior sound architecture those scripts render.

**Deployments:** Chapter 13 §13.3 and Appendix Part 3 §3.6 — support the distinction between the durable epigraphic record and the origin of the Sanskrit sound-and-script architecture.

Richard Salomon's survey of Indian epigraphy classifies inscriptions by content as donative, dedicatory, panegyric, ritual, literary, administrative, and private, and by medium across stone, metal, pottery, and other materials. The Archaeological Survey of India likewise notes that many stone inscriptions are donative rather than royal. Early Brāhmī also survives in cave donations, seals, coins, graffiti, and inscribed potsherds. Royal inscriptions remain unusually prominent because rulers could command monumental surfaces and skilled labour, but the surviving archive cannot be reduced to royal speech.

The methodological point in the appendix is narrower. Stone and metal survive more readily than palm leaf, birch bark, wood, and cloth. The earliest surviving durable inscription can therefore date that artifact and its written form. It cannot by itself date the oral analysis of the sounds, the वर्णमाला (*varṇamālā*) that orders them, or the अक्षर (*akṣara*) that the script renders.

Sources: Richard Salomon, [*Indian Epigraphy: A Guide to the Study of Inscriptions in Sanskrit, Prakrit, and the Other Indo-Aryan Languages*](https://academic.oup.com/book/49774) (Oxford University Press, 1998), especially Chapter 2; Archaeological Survey of India, [BharatSHRI, “About Epigraphy”](https://bharatshri.asi.gov.in/AboutEpigraphy?lang=en); Tamil Nadu Department of Archaeology, [“Tamil-Brahmi Script”](https://www.tnarch.gov.in/tamil-brahmi-script).

### `writing-medium-fire-and-authority`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- unesco-nalanda-nomination-1502 | Nalanda's recorded library complex, evidence of conflagration, invasion, arson, and the loss of records
- plutarch-caesar-49-library-fire | ancient account of the Alexandrian fire reaching the library
- cassius-dio-42-38-books-fire | ancient account of Alexandrian storehouses and books burning
- openlearn-alexandria-library-loss | modern synthesis of fire, later destruction, and gradual loss at Alexandria
- bukhari-4987-uthman-copies | order to burn other Qur'anic materials after distributing committee-prepared copies
-->

**Short:** Written collections can disappear through decay, fire, war, or command. Nalanda and Alexandria show the vulnerability of knowledge gathered in destructible objects at physical sites. The account of ʿUthmān supplies a direct example of authority distributing one text and ordering other Qur'anic materials, including complete copies, burned.

**Deployments:** Chapter 13 §13.3 — historical evidence for the claim that लिपि (*lipi*) can support a distributed calibrant but cannot reliably become its primary medium.

The Nalanda nomination dossier prepared by the Archaeological Survey of India records the library buildings named Ratnaranjak, Ratnasagara, and Ratnadodhi and the innumerable manuscripts they were said to contain. It also records archaeological evidence of conflagration, invasion and arson, structures destroyed by fire, and the site's eventual abandonment. No archaeological remains of the library buildings have been identified. The evidence therefore establishes that Nalanda's accumulated written record was vulnerable to the destruction of its physical site without requiring the popular claim that one fire burned every manuscript for months.

For Alexandria, Plutarch's *Life of Caesar* 49.6 reports that the fire begun at the dockyards spread and destroyed the great library. Cassius Dio 42.38.2 records storehouses of grain and books burning during the same fighting. Modern histories separate this fire from later losses at the Serapeum and from the long effects of neglect. Chapter 13 therefore makes the narrower claim that written collections at Alexandria burned. It does not reduce the disappearance of the Alexandrian libraries to one fire.

Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4987 records a different failure mode. Caliph ʿUthmān appointed Zayd ibn Thābit and three Qurayshi men to prepare copies, sent those copies to the provinces, and ordered the remaining Qur'anic materials burned, whether fragments or complete manuscripts. Here the physical medium gave central authority a direct means of eliminating competing written copies.

The three examples have different histories and motives. Their shared architectural lesson is straightforward: when knowledge depends upon destructible objects, fire can consume it, war can scatter it, and authority can decide which copies survive. A distributed aural calibrant removes that single point of failure.

Sources: Archaeological Survey of India and Ministry of Culture, Government of India, [*The Archaeological Site of Nalanda Mahavihara at Nalanda, Bihar: Nomination Dossier*](https://whc.unesco.org/uploads/nominations/1502.pdf), Chapter 2, pp. 19, 21, 43, and 84; Plutarch, [*Life of Caesar* 49.6](https://penelope.uchicago.edu/Thayer/E/Roman/Texts/Plutarch/Lives/Caesar%2A.html); Cassius Dio, [*Roman History* 42.38.2](https://penelope.uchicago.edu/Thayer/E/Roman/Texts/Cassius_Dio/42%2A.html); Open University, [“The Library of Alexandria,” §3, “The End of the Library”](https://www.open.edu/openlearn/history-the-arts/library-alexandria/content-section-3/); [*Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* 4987](https://sunnah.com/bukhari:4987).

### `daniels-abjad-abugida-typology`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- daniels-fundamentals-grammatology-1990 | introduction and definition of abjad and abugida
- unicode-standard-chapter-14-brahmi | Unicode's application of abugida to Brāhmī
-->

**Short:** Peter T. Daniels introduced *abjad* and *abugida* as script-type names in “Fundamentals of Grammatology” (1990). *Abugida* derives from the first four signs of the Ethiopic script in Semitic order and denotes a system in which consonant signs carry an inherent vowel that other marks modify or suppress.

**Deployments:** Appendix Part 3 §3.7 — supports the history and defined scope of the term *abugida*; §3.10 — supplies a standard history-of-writing reference for the corridor sequence the appendix contests.

Daniels coined *abjad* for consonant-centred writing systems and *abugida* for systems whose consonant signs carry an inherent vowel modified by additional marks. The Unicode Standard uses the latter category for Brāhmī and its descendants. The appendix accepts that classification at the level it describes: the behaviour of signs. Its separate term *audiography* identifies a different feature, namely that an Indic script renders a prior, systematic analysis of articulated sound.

Daniels and William Bright's *The World's Writing Systems* also exemplifies the standard history-of-writing sequence through ancient Near-Eastern systems, West Semitic scripts, Greek, and later alphabets before treating the scripts of the Indian subcontinent. The source supports the existence of that conventional organization. The appendix's claim that the sequence serves a foundational civilizational dogma is the book's analysis.

Sources: Peter T. Daniels, [“Fundamentals of Grammatology”](https://doi.org/10.2307/602899), *Journal of the American Oriental Society* 110, no. 4 (1990): 727–731; Peter T. Daniels and William Bright, eds., *The World's Writing Systems* (Oxford University Press, 1996); Unicode Consortium, [Glossary, “Abugida”](https://unicode.org/glossary/), and [*The Unicode Standard*, Chapter 6, “Writing Systems”](https://www.unicode.org/versions/latest/ch06.pdf).

### `aksara-imperishable-name`

**Short:** अक्षर (*akṣara*) = अ- (*a-*, privative) + the धातुः (*dhātuḥ*) ⟪क्षर्⟫ (*kṣar*, to flow, to perish) — *the imperishable*. The term can denote a syllabic sound-unit, a written character, or the metaphysical imperishable. This book uses it first for the stable pronounced unit and calls the visible form through which a script renders that unit an *audiograph*.

**Deployments:** Chapter 9 §9.6 (the *akṣara* introduction); Appendix Part 3 §§3.1 and 3.7 (the distinction between the pronounced *akṣara* and the audiograph that renders it).

The Sanskrit term अक्षर (*akṣara*) is formed from अ- (*a-*, privative) + the धातुः (*dhātuḥ*) ⟪क्षर्⟫ (*kṣar*, to flow, to perish, to wear away) — literally *that which does not flow away* or *that which does not decay*. Its semantic range includes the syllabic unit of utterance, the written character that renders such a unit, and the Upaniṣadic and Vedāntic term for the imperishable itself. Bhagavad Gītā 8.3 gives **अक्षरं ब्रह्म परमं (*akṣaraṃ brahma paramaṃ*)**, the supreme imperishable Brahman. Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad 1.1.5 identifies higher knowledge as that by which the imperishable is known. Ṛgveda 1.164.39 already places the अक्षर (*akṣara*) in the highest space where the देवाः (*devāḥ*) are assembled; see endnote `rigveda-1-164-39-akshara-assembly`.

The book's distinction does not deny that अक्षर (*akṣara*) can refer to a written character. It identifies the architectural sequence. The pronounced unit exists before a script records it; लिपि (*lipi*) then gives it visible form. *Audiograph* denotes that visible rendering so that the sound-unit and its written interface do not collapse into one object.

The morphological grounding follows the transparent privative formation and the standard Sanskrit lexica. See also endnote `rigveda-1-164-39-akshara-assembly` for the Vedic deployment of the term.

---

### `chronology-asymmetry-rationale`

**Short:** The book uses exact dates when a dated publication, appointment, commission, inscription, or other documentary event establishes a sequence needed by the argument. It does not repeat disputed dates for Indic texts and figures when the date contributes nothing to the architectural analysis.

**Deployments:** Preface ¶8 — the closing line of the chronology section that flags the asymmetry as deliberate rather than as inconsistent mistrust.

This is an editorial method, not a claim that one civilization records time honestly and another does not. A dated nineteenth-century book or university appointment can be placed in sequence from its own publication or institutional record. The proposed dates of the Vedas, Pāṇini, Yāska, and other Indic texts or figures are different kinds of claims. They depend upon historical models that this book examines rather than assumes.

The book therefore distinguishes a documented event from a reconstructed date. It gives the year when the year does useful work. When an exact date remains disputed or contributes nothing to the argument, it describes the depth of time without pretending that the dispute has been settled. The Epilogue explains why this refusal does not require the book to construct a replacement chronology.

---

### `vedanta-anta-chronology-capture`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- vhp-upanishads | section “Nature of Upanishads”
- gita-supersite-15-15 | Bhagavad Gita 15.15
-->

**Short:** ***Vedānta*** identifies the conclusion, culmination, or goal of Vedic knowledge; that relationship does not establish a date of composition. The Government of India's Vedic Heritage Portal states both claims in sequence: it defines *Vedānta* as the conclusion and goal of the Vedas, then says the Upaniṣads came chronologically at the end of a *Vedic period*.

**Deployments:** Chapter 0 §0.5.

**Deployment:** Chapter 0 §0.5, “When *Anta* Becomes a Date.”

Three meanings must remain separate:

1. **Structural position.** The Upaniṣads occur within the Vedic corpus and are traditionally associated with its concluding or culminating instruction.
2. **Purpose or goal.** *Anta* can identify the culmination or highest purpose of Vedic knowledge. The Bhagavad Gītā 15.15 uses ***वेदान्तकृत् (*vedāntakṛt*)*** within a statement about what the Vedas disclose and who knows them; it does not name an historical period.
3. **Chronological claim.** The proposition that the Upaniṣads were composed after the Saṃhitās, Brāhmaṇas, and Āraṇyakas requires independent evidence. It does not follow from either structural position or philosophical culmination.

The institutional example shows how easily these meanings are merged. The Vedic Heritage Portal first says that *Vedānta* means the end, conclusion, and goal of the Vedas. Its next sentence says that the Upaniṣads came chronologically at the end of a *Vedic period*. The page places the structural definition beside the chronological claim without demonstrating that one establishes the other.

The book's argument does not claim that textual arrangement supplies no historical information. It claims that arrangement, purpose, style, and chronology are different kinds of information. A date must be established through evidence connected to time rather than inferred from the word *anta* or from a composition's philosophical and literary style.

Sources: Government of India, Vedic Heritage Portal, [“Upanishads”](https://vedicheritage.gov.in/upanishads/), under “Nature of Upanishads”; Bhagavad Gītā 15.15, [Gītā Supersite, IIT Kanpur](https://www.gitasupersite.iitk.ac.in/srimad?choose=1&etadi=1&etgb=1&etpurohit=1&etsiva=1&etssa=1&field_chapter_value=15&field_nsutra_value=15&language=dv&show_mool=1).

### `vedanta-textual-placement`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- vhp-vedic-text-placement | Aitareya Aranyaka; Taittiriya Aranyaka; Vajasaneyi Kanva Samhita chapter 40
-->

**Short:** The Upaniṣads do not occupy one uniform final layer across the Vedic corpus. The Aitareya Upaniṣad appears inside the second of the Aitareya Āraṇyaka's five divisions, while the Īśopaniṣad forms chapter 40 of the Vājasaneyī Saṃhitā. Their textual placement breaks the neat Saṃhitā → Brāhmaṇa → Āraṇyaka → Upaniṣad sequence often converted into chronology.

**Deployments:** Chapter 0 §0.5.

**Deployment:** Chapter 0 §0.5, “When *Anta* Becomes a Date.”

The Aitareya Āraṇyaka contains five major divisions. Chapters 4, 5, and 6 of its second division form the Aitareya Upaniṣad; the third, fourth, and fifth divisions follow it. This is a direct example of an Upaniṣad placed within a larger Vedic text rather than at its physical end.

The Īśopaniṣad occupies chapter 40 of the Vājasaneyī Saṃhitā of the Śukla Yajurveda. It stands at the end of that Saṃhitā, but it does not follow a separately arranged Brāhmaṇa and Āraṇyaka in the neat four-stage sequence imposed upon the complete Vedic corpus.

The Taittirīya Āraṇyaka provides a third arrangement. It contains ten *prapāṭhakas*. The seventh, eighth, and ninth form the Taittirīya Upaniṣad, and the tenth is the Mahānārāyaṇa Upaniṣad. The Taittirīya example shows why the title of one Upaniṣad cannot be treated as a marker placed at the physical end of every Vedic arrangement.

These placements do not establish dates. They show that Saṃhitā, Brāhmaṇa, Āraṇyaka, and Upaniṣad do not form one universal table of contents that can be converted into a historical timeline.

Sources: Government of India, Vedic Heritage Portal, [“Aitareya Āraṇyaka”](https://vedicheritage.gov.in/aranyakas/aitareyaranyaka/), [“Taittirīya Āraṇyaka”](https://vedicheritage.gov.in/aranyakas/taittiriya-aranyaka/), and [“Vājasaneyī Kāṇva Saṃhitā, Chapter 40”](https://vedicheritage.gov.in/hi/samhitas/yajurveda/vajasaneyi-kanva-samhita/vajasaneyi-kanva-samhita-chapter-40/).

### `ramayana-homer-chronology-capture`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- weber-ramayana-1873-scan | printed pp. 22-24
- telang-ramayana-homer-1873-metadata | title and publication record
- west-indo-european-poetry-myth-2007 | pp. 12-14 and 437-438
-->

**Short:** Nineteenth-century Indology used its chronology to place Homer before Vālmīki and then proposed that Sītā's abduction and the war at Laṅkā were modeled on Helen's abduction and the siege of Troy. Later Indo-European comparison replaced direct copying with reconstructed common inheritance, but it continued to place an external source above both epics.

**Deployments:** Chapter 3 §3.4.

**Deployment:** Chapter 3 §3.4, after the progress pillar exposes the civilizational function of imposed chronology.

The direct-borrowing claim is explicit rather than inferred. In **Albrecht Weber's** *"On the Rāmāyaṇa,"* published in English in *The Indian Antiquary* 1 (1872), Weber wrote that the abduction of Helen and the siege of Troy had served as the model for the corresponding incidents in Vālmīki's poem. He did not claim that Vālmīki had read Homer. He proposed that knowledge of the Homeric story reached India through contact following Alexander's expedition. The paper therefore required a *Rāmāyaṇa* late enough to receive Greek material. **Kashinath Trimbak Telang** answered Weber directly in *Was the Râmâyaṇa Copied from Homer? A Reply to Professor Weber* (Bombay, 1873). Telang deserves credit for recognizing and challenging the direction imposed upon the comparison while the theory was still being advanced.

The chronological frame survived the dispute. Western philology commonly places Homeric epic in the eighth or seventh century BCE, Pāṇini in approximately the fifth or fourth century BCE, and the Sanskrit epics across a broad period beginning around 400 BCE. The individual estimates vary, but their ordering performs the same cultural operation: Homer appears first; Pāṇini and the text classified as *"Classical Sanskrit"* appear later. Modern scholarship often acknowledges much older oral and narrative depth within the Indian epics, yet continues to place the recoverable Sanskrit composition inside that imposed sequence.

The later explanation is softer than Weber's copying charge. M. L. West's *Indo-European Poetry and Myth* (Oxford University Press, 2007), for example, treats Indic and Greek parallels through reconstructed Indo-European poetic inheritance. In discussing abduction and recovery narratives, West cites the proposal that Greek and Indic episodes preserve an inherited Indo-European narrative pattern, while adding qualifications of his own. This framework no longer needs Vālmīki to copy Homer directly. It places both beneath a reconstructed people and their reconstructed narrative inheritance and their reconstructed narrative inheritance.

The sources establish three facts: the imposed order of dates, Weber's explicit Homer-to-Vālmīki borrowing claim, and the later common-inheritance framework. The claim that this sequence protects Greek cultural priority is this book's diagnosis of what the arrangement accomplishes; it is not presented as a confession found in those sources.

Sources: Albrecht Weber, [*"On the Rāmāyaṇa"*](https://www.wisdomlib.org/history/book/indian-antiquary-journal/d/doc1897639.html), *The Indian Antiquary* 1 (1872), especially the discussion of Helen, Troy, Sītā, and Laṅkā; Kashinath Trimbak Telang, [*Was the Râmâyaṇa Copied from Homer? A Reply to Professor Weber*](https://openlibrary.org/books/OL5971044M/Was_the_Ra%CC%82ma%CC%82yan%CC%A3a_copied_from_Homer) (Bombay: Union Press, 1873); M. L. West, [*Indo-European Poetry and Myth*](https://academic.oup.com/book/10022) (Oxford University Press, 2007), especially pp. 12–14 and 437–38.

### `indo-european-narrative-inheritance`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- west-indo-european-poetry-myth-2007 | pp. 166–172, 186–191, and 255–260
- watkins-how-to-kill-a-dragon-1995 | Hero-Slays-Serpent formula and chapters
-->

**Short:** Comparative Indo-European mythology constructs hypothetical ancestral versions of real Vedic figures and narratives: **द्यौष्पिता (*Dyauṣ Pitā*)** becomes reconstructed Father Sky; **अश्विनौ (*Aśvinau*)** become reconstructed Divine Twins or Twin Horsemen; and Indra **वृत्रहन् (*Vṛtrahan*)** becomes one instance of a reconstructed Serpent-Slayer story. The Vedic figures are preserved in the Veda; the supposed common ancestral people and their stories are modern reconstructions.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.2.

**Deployment:** Chapter 19 §19.2, where imaginary people, language, and words expand into imaginary ancestral stories.

The three Sanskrit anchors are real features of the Vedic corpus. **द्यौष्पिता (*Dyauṣ Pitā*)**, Father Sky, joins the sky deity with the father relation. **अश्विनौ (*Aśvinau*)**, the two Aśvins, are the Vedic horsemen and rescuing twins. **वृत्रहन् (*Vṛtrahan*)** is one of Indra's standing epithets: the one who struck down Vṛtra and released the obstructed waters. Their presence in the Veda does not depend on a reconstruction.

Comparative Indo-European mythology reverses that evidentiary order. It compares Dyauṣ with Zeus and Jupiter and reconstructs **\*Dyēus ph₂tēr** as an ancestral Father Sky. It compares the Aśvins with the Greek Dioscuri and other paired rescuers and reconstructs ancestral Divine Twins, often represented as horsemen and sons of Father Sky. It compares Indra's defeat of Vṛtra with Greek, Iranian, Germanic, and other serpent-slaying accounts and reconstructs a common Hero-Slays-Serpent formula or story-pattern.

M. L. West's *Indo-European Poetry and Myth* presents all three operations in one modern synthesis: Father Sky and the children of reconstructed *Dyeus* at pp. 166–72; the Vedic Aśvins, Greek Dioscuri, and proposed ancestral Divine Twins at pp. 186–91; and Indra's defeat of Vṛtra within a reconstructed Indo-European serpent-slaying complex at pp. 255–60. Calvert Watkins develops the last reconstruction across *How to Kill a Dragon: Aspects of Indo-European Poetics* (Oxford University Press, 1995), including the proposed Hero-Slays-Serpent formula.

The distinction in Chapter 19 is therefore exact. The Veda preserves Dyauṣ Pitā, the Aśvins, Indra, Vṛtra, and the relevant mantras. Greek and other corpora preserve their own figures and narratives. No surviving record preserves a common ancestral corpus in which reconstructed Indo-European people told reconstructed versions of all three. Comparative mythology produces those ancestral versions by inference and then places the recorded Vedic material inside the resulting inheritance. Chapter 19 treats that procedure as the story-level extension of PIE: imaginary people speaking an imaginary language are supplied with imaginary ancestral stories.

Sources: M. L. West, [*Indo-European Poetry and Myth*](https://academic.oup.com/book/10022) (Oxford University Press, 2007), especially pp. 166–72, 186–91, and 255–60; Calvert Watkins, [*How to Kill a Dragon: Aspects of Indo-European Poetics*](https://academic.oup.com/book/47065) (Oxford University Press, 1995), especially the Hero-Slays-Serpent formula and the Serpent-Slayer chapters.

### `satyam-bhutahitam-mahabharata`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- ambuda-mahabharata-3-200 | Vana Parva 3.200.3–4 Sanskrit text and narrative context
-->

**Short:** Mahābhārata, Vana Parva 3.200.4 states: *yad bhūta-hitam atyantaṃ tat satyam iti dhāraṇā* — “that which is fully and ultimately beneficial to beings is held to be truth.” Numbering varies across editions, but the Sanskrit verse and its Dharma-vyādha context are verified.

**Deployments:** Chapter 0 §0.7 — the *laukika* test for सत्, paired with the *vaidika* grounding the section builds from the ऋत-family and RV citations; Part I opener — now a callback to §0.7 rather than the reader's first encounter with the standard; Epilogue §The Contest of Architectures — the standard that explains why *āryatva* is desirable.

The active citation uses the unsandhied form of the Mahābhārata's ethical definition of truth:

> यद्भूतहितमत्यन्तं तत्सत्यमिति धारणा ।
> विपर्ययकृतोऽधर्मः पश्य धर्मस्य सूक्ष्मताम् ॥
>
> *yad bhūta-hitam atyantaṃ tat satyam iti dhāraṇā |*
> *viparyaya-kṛto 'dharmaḥ paśya dharmasya sūkṣmatām ||*

The verse appears within the Dharma-vyādha's teaching. The preceding verse explains that truth and untruth cannot be assigned mechanically: under grave circumstances, an untruth may function as truth and a truth as untruth. Verse 4 then supplies the criterion of the welfare of beings and warns that the contrary produces *adharma*. Chapter 0 uses this as a *laukika* test for applying the older distinction between सत् (*sat*) and असत् (*asat*).

The key expression is **भूतहित (*bhūta-hita*)**, the welfare or good of beings. The compound is broader than a test restricted to one faction or inherited identity. How far a particular application extends must still be argued from its context; the verse supplies the criterion, not an automatic verdict for every case.

Source: [Mahābhārata 3.200.3–4](https://ambuda.org/texts/mahabharatam/3.200), Ambuda Sanskrit edition. Some editions number the same passage differently; the note therefore identifies both the parvan and the verified digital locator rather than implying that one numbering system applies to every edition.

### `nanartha-homonymy`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- grassmann-rigveda-dictionary-1873 | s.v. aja, p. 19
- cologne-mw-1899 | entries aj, aja, and a-ja, p. 9
- aussant-homonymy-polysemy-2015 | pp. 13–36
-->

**Short:** Sanskrit's lexicographers catalogued both polysemy and homonymy. The *Amarakośa* contains a dedicated *nānārtha-varga*, and the analytical tradition distinguished one word expressing several related senses from several words sharing one sound-form.

**Deployments:** Parked — no current manuscript deployment.

**Status:** Parked — Chapter 3 §3.6 no longer uses homonymy to assign individual Rigvedic occurrences of *asura*. Retained for its general account of Sanskrit's lexical categories.

**नानार्थ (*nānārtha*)** — "many-meaning" — is the standing lexicographic category for a single phonic form bearing more than one referent. The category is not a modern reconstruction; it is the discipline's own, worked out in the thesauri and the grammatical-philosophical literature.

The **अमरकोश (*Amarakośa*)** — Amarasiṃha's thesaurus, the most widely memorized lexicon in the lineage-chain — collects such forms in a dedicated section, the **नानार्थवर्ग (*nānārtha-varga*)**, one of the five vargas of the third *kāṇḍa* (the *Sāmānyādi-kāṇḍa*). The section's own organization confirms the point: where the rest of the lexicon arranges words by *meaning-domain*, the *nānārtha-varga* arranges them by *form* — by final consonant, from *kānta* (forms ending in -*ka*) through the forms ending in -*ha*. A section indexed by shape rather than sense is a section built for exactly the phenomenon at issue: one shape, several senses or several words.

Worked examples the tradition itself uses:

- **गो (*go*)** — the classic *nānārtha* form, discussed by Yāska in the *Nirukta*. Senses include cow, the earth, speech (*vāc*), and the ray or beam of light, among others (Monier-Williams, s.v. *go*, lists these and more). One form, many referents; the reciter disambiguates by context.
- **हरि (*hari*)** — senses include Viṣṇu, Indra, the sun, the moon, a lion, a horse, a monkey, and the color green/tawny (Monier-Williams, s.v. *hari*). A stock entry in the *nānārtha* lists.
- **अज (*aja*)** — Grassmann's concordance separates three entries under this one sound-form: *aja*, driver; *aja*, goat; and *a-ja*, unborn. Monier-Williams likewise places the non-privative entry beside ⟪अज्⟫ (*aj*, to drive), then gives *a-ja* as a separate privative entry. The lexical evidence supports the chapter's contrast without requiring the additional claim that "goat" literally means "the driven one." The Gītā's *ajo nityaḥ śāśvato 'yam* at 2.20 supplies the familiar use of *a-ja*, the Unborn.

The mechanism was itself debated: whether a *nānārtha* form is **one word with many meanings** (***ekaśabdadarśana***, the single-word view — polysemy) or **many words sharing a form** (***anekaśabdadarśana***, the many-words view — homonymy). The terminology is the Sanskrit analytical tradition's own, surveyed in Émilie Aussant, "Sanskrit Theories on Homonymy and Polysemy," *Bulletin d'Études Indiennes* 32 (2015), pp. 13–36. Aussant's contrast between connected and unconnected senses explains the distinction.

Chapter 3 no longer classifies *asura* as an *anekaśabda* case. Yāska's life-breath analysis and the Kauthuma division ***a + surasya*** establish two available analyses, but neither source permits every Rigvedic occurrence to be assigned to one of them.

Sources: Hermann Grassmann, *Wörterbuch zum Rig-Veda* (Leipzig, 1873), s.v. *aja*, p. 19; Monier Monier-Williams, *A Sanskrit-English Dictionary* (Oxford, 1899), s.v. ⟪अज्⟫, *aja*, and *a-ja*, p. 9; Émilie Aussant, article cited above.

### `sura-dhatu-dipti`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- project-dhatupatha-csv | base index 06.0066
- ashtadhyayi-rule-cluster-digital | Dhātupāṭha 6.66
- cologne-mw-1899 | entries sura and asura
- eggeling-satapatha-vol44-1900 | Satapatha Brahmana 11.1.6.7–8, pp. 13–14
-->

**Short:** The inherited *Dhātupāṭha* lists ⟪षुर्⟫, which appears as ⟪सुर्⟫, with the meanings **ऐश्वर्यदीप्त्योः** — "of sovereignty and of shining." The **दीप्ति (*dīpti*)** meaning supports the book's diagnostic ***a-sura***, the figure opposed to radiance.

**Deployments:** Chapter 3 §3.6 — the radiant word-family and the book's ***a-sura*** derivation.

**The dhātu in the inherited list.** The *dhātu* is ⟪सुर्⟫, cited in aupadeśika form as **षुरँ (*ṣur*)**: the initial ष् appears as स्, while the final ँ is an accent marker. The entry appears in the *tudādi gaṇa* at *Dhātupāṭha* 6.66 (ashtadhyayi.com base-index `06.0066`). Its *artha* is **ऐश्वर्यदीप्त्योः (*aiśvarya-dīptyoḥ*)**, the genitive dual "of sovereignty and of shining." Ashtadhyayi.com renders the two meanings as "to rule, to be powerful, to shine" and *ऐश्वर्यवान् होना, प्रकाशित होना*. The **दीप्ति (*dīpti*)** sense therefore appears in the inherited inventory itself.

**The diagnostic used by the book.** The *dīpti* sense supports ***sura***, radiant, and Sanskrit's privative ***a-*** forms ***a-sura***, the figure opposed to radiance. The operation follows the same familiar pattern as ***a-hiṃsā, a-dharma,*** and ***a-sita***. The Kauthuma Sāmaveda Padapāṭha records the division directly when it separates ***asurasya*** as ***a + surasya***. This establishes the availability of the privative analysis. It does not identify which analysis belongs to each Rigvedic occurrence.

**The dictionary objection.** Monier-Williams treats the noun *sura* as a probable back-formation from *asura*: a positive created by treating the initial *a-* as privative, "as *sita* from *a-sita*." That proposal concerns the independent uses of the noun found in the corpus. It does not remove ⟪सुर्⟫ from the inherited *Dhātupāṭha*, where *dīpti* is explicit, and MW's own comparison still places the pair inside a bright / not-bright pattern. Chapter 3 rejects the dictionary's demand that an independently recorded noun must precede a form that Sanskrit can generate.

**What the machinery did with it.** Monier-Williams records the semantic atom सुर् as "to shine," citing the *Dhātupāṭha*. In a separate entry, the dictionary treats the noun *sura* as a back-formation from *asura*. Comparative philology then builds the Indo-Iranian reversal around *deva/asura* and *daeva/ahura*. The references keep the semantic atom, noun, and privative operation apart. Chapter 3 brings them together to establish the available Sanskrit formation सुर् (*to shine*) → *sura* (radiant) → *a-sura* (opposed to radiance). That formation does not assign a division to any particular Rigvedic occurrence.

**Reception of the distinction.** A modern WisdomLib rendering on RV 2.1.6 distinguishes the praised title from an "A-Sura" or "Anti-Shining" interpretation. This shows that the privative reading exists within modern reception; it does not establish the division of any particular Rigvedic occurrence. Svarbhānu's light-bearing name and his act of darkening Sūrya make the reading especially resonant without turning that resonance into lexical proof (see `svarbhanu-svar-etymology`).

**The later corpus attaches darkness to the contrast.** Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa 11.1.6.7–8 sorts the creation by breath and by light: "By (the breath of) his mouth he created the gods… Having created them, there was, as it were, **daylight** for him. And by the downward breathing he created the Asuras… Having created them there was, as it were, **darkness** for him." This is Julius Eggeling's translation in *Sacred Books of the East*, vol. 44 (Oxford, 1900), pp. 13–14. The passage supplies a later light-and-darkness contrast; it does not divide the Rigvedic word. The Taittirīya Brāhmaṇa preserves a parallel creation sequence at 2.2.9.5–8 and the *su-/asu-* word-play at 2.3.8.2 and 4. The frequently repeated "lower breath" dictionary sentence is not present in the 1899 Monier-Williams entry and is therefore not used as evidence here.

**A distinction kept, to forestall the philologist's counter.** The book's *a-sura* = "not-light" is built on the dhātu सुर् (*dīpti*). It does not rest on — and must not be conflated with — सूर (*sūra*, long *ū*, "the sun"), स्वर् (*svar*, the light-realm), which are cognate light-words, nor with सुरा (*surā*, "liquor," from ⟪सु⟫ *su*, "to distil"), which is unrelated.

**Etymology-independent backstop.** The containment diagnosis does not depend on assigning either analysis to a particular Rigvedic occurrence. Chapter 3 follows the deed itself: protection and release on one side, concealment and obstruction on the other.

Source: ashtadhyayi.com *Dhātupāṭha*, entry `06.0066` (*tudādi gaṇa*), cross-checked against the local machine-readable inventory in `analysis/dhatupatha/` (gaṇa 6, entry 66 = षुरँ). Monier-Williams s.v. *sura* / *asura* for the back-formation account. The note credits the inherited grammatical continuum for the inventory and Pāṇini for the documentation that presupposes it.

---

### `s-mobile-root-extension-confessions`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- shields-s-mobile-1996 | pp. 249–250 and 253
- clackson-ie-linguistics-2007 | pp. 67–70, especially p. 68
- pokorny-iew-1959-vol1 | pp. 938–945
-->

**Short:** The parenthesized *s* in \*(s)ker- records *s-mobile*: related forms preserve the initial sound inconsistently, under conditions that scholarship has not defined precisely. Competing explanations include sandhi loss, an obscured prefix, and mistaken word division. The *t* in \*(s)ker-t- is treated as a root extension, but the reconstructed extension has no identified semantic or morphological function. Pokorny lists the cutting family and its extended forms at pp. 938–45.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.5 (the Recipe — move 3, the notational confessions; move 6, the *t*).

Kenneth Shields's review of *s-mobile* opens with T. Burrow's description of initial *s* as unstable under conditions that scholars had not been able to define accurately. Shields then surveys three explanations: sandhi loss, a prefix whose function became obscured, and mistaken word division. He favors reinterpretation at a word boundary but concludes that the origin will probably never be known definitively. The literature therefore records a sporadic alternation and competing historical explanations, not a conditioning rule that predicts every occurrence. Kenneth Shields Jr., "Indo-European S-Mobile and Indo-European Morphology," *Emerita* 64.2 (1996), pp. 249–54, especially pp. 249–50 and 253.

The **root extension** (German *Erweiterung*, also "enlargement" or "determinative") belongs to a reconstructed analysis of longer bases. James Clackson explains that scholars object to these extensions because no particular function can be assigned to them; in many cases the analysis supplies no semantic or morphological explanation for the reconstructed form. He does not call every extension meaningless or ruleless. *Indo-European Linguistics: An Introduction* (Cambridge University Press, 2007), pp. 67–70, especially p. 68. Julius Pokorny's *Indogermanisches etymologisches Wörterbuch* (Francke, 1959), pp. 938–45, organizes the cutting family as 4. *(s)ker-*, with extended forms including *(s)kret-*. These sources support the chapter's narrower claim: the notation records uncertainty about the initial *s*, while the added *t* has no identified function.

### `krt-dhatupatha-chedane`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- sanskritdocuments-dhatupatha-index | entries 6.171 and 7.10
- project-dhatupatha-csv | base index 06.0171
-->

**Short:** ⟪कृत्⟫ (*kṛt*), aupadeśika कृतीँ, *tudādi* — Dhātupāṭha 6.171, *artha* **छेदने** ("in cutting"; *kṛntati*, "he cuts") — verified against the machine-readable Pāṇinian Dhātupāṭha. The homonymous कृत् 7.10 (*rudhādi*, वेष्टने "to surround") is a distinct entry and plays no part in the cut-family.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.5 (the Recipe — move 4, the atom).

The entry data: base-index 06.0171, dhātu कृत्, aupadeśika कृतीँ, gaṇa 6 (*tudādi*), pada P, artha छेदने, meaning "to cut, to break, to slice." Present *kṛntati*; participle *kṛtta* (*kṛt + kta*), "cut off" — the pair Chapter 19 sets beside Latin *curtus*. Derivatives in the near orbit include *kartana* (cutting), *kartarī* (scissors), and *kṛntana*. The SanskritDocuments index independently prints **कृत् । तु० सेट् प० । कृतीँ छेदने ६.१७१** and the distinct homonym **कृत् । रु० सेट् प० । कृतीँ वेष्टने ७.१०**. The similarly written 10.155 entry begins with long **कॄत्**, not **कृत्**.

### `krt-upasarga-corpus`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- project-path-c-valency-csv | kṛt aggregate: 650 tokens and 19 prefix labels
- project-attestation-index-csv | prefix counts for normalized kṛt
- dcs-sanskrit-repository-04e0778 | underlying corpus described in the analysis README
-->

**Short:** The book's Digital Corpus of Sanskrit analysis normalizes **650 tokens** under the base string **kṛt** and records **19 prefix labels**, including the null label and eighteen non-null labels. No row records an inserted *s* immediately before **kṛt**. The aggregation does not distinguish the homonymous Dhātupāṭha entries, so it cannot by itself assign all 650 tokens specifically to the cutting atom at 6.171.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.5 (the Recipe — the corpus sentence in the *s*-paragraph).

The aggregation comes from the book's reproducibility bundle: `analysis/ganah/data/derived/path_c_valency.csv` records **kṛt** with valency 74, 650 tokens, and 19 distinct prefix labels including the null label. `analysis/ganah/data/derived/attestation_index.csv` supplies these prefix totals: *ni* 223, null 220, *ut* 74, *api* 28, *vi* 15, *ava* 15, *vini* 14, *sam* 14, *pari* 13, *pra* 9, *apa* 8, *abhi* 3, *samut* 3, *saṃni* 3, *anu* 2, *upa* 2, *vyava* 2, *saṃpra* 1, and *ā* 1. The DCS source and processing are documented in `analysis/ganah/README.md`.

Path C groups records by normalized atom string. It therefore combines homonymous entries written **kṛt** and cannot prove that every token belongs to the cutting entry at Dhātupāṭha 6.171. The data do support the narrower statements that the normalized **kṛt** group occurs with eighteen non-null prefix labels and that none of its rows inserts *s* immediately before the atom. The Chapter 19 sentence currently makes the stronger homonym-specific claim and is flagged for author review.

### `sut-agama-visarga-s`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- ashtadhyayi-rule-cluster-digital | 6.1.135 and 6.1.137–139; 8.3.46 excluded
-->

**Short:** Pāṇini's *suṭ* cluster states where an inserted *s* appears. Rule 6.1.135 places *suṭ* immediately before *k*; 6.1.137 specifies *sam-*, *pari-*, and *upa-* before the *kṛ* family in the sense of *bhūṣaṇa*; 6.1.138 adds *samavāya*; and 6.1.139 specifies further uses after *upa-*. The cluster generates forms including **saṃ-s-kṛta** and **pariṣ-kṛta**. It does not apply to the distinct cutting atom ⟪कृत्⟫.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.5 (the Recipe — the *s*-paragraph: the suṭ, the refusal, and the ear's *skṛt-*).

The rule text and the Kāśikā give: 6.1.135 *suṭ kāt pūrvaḥ*; 6.1.137 *saṃparyupebhyaḥ karotau bhūṣaṇe*; 6.1.138 *samavāye ca*; and 6.1.139 the further *upa-* rule beginning *upāt prat...*. The crucial correction is that 6.1.137 lists three preceding forms — *sam*, *pari*, and *upa* — not two. Rule 8.3.46 applies to a separate visarga context and does not support the earlier version of this note: its examples include *ayaskāra* and *payaskāra*, while the Kāśikā assigns *bhāskara* elsewhere. Those compounds have therefore been removed from the claim. Sources: the *Aṣṭādhyāyī* and Kāśikā on 6.1.135 and 6.1.137–39, SanskritDocuments explanatory text, cross-checked against ashtadhyayi.com; see also the separate corpus check in `krt-upasarga-corpus`.

### `jan-dhatupatha-double-entry`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- project-dhatupatha-csv | base indices 03.0025 and 04.0044
- ashtadhyayi-rule-cluster-digital | Kasika on 7.3.79
-->

**Short:** The Dhātupāṭha preserves ⟪जन्⟫ in two entries, covering both meanings assigned to \*ǵenh₁: जनँ, *juhotyādi*, **जनने** — "to create or procreate"; and जनीँ, *divādi*, **प्रादुर्भावे** — "to be born or come into existence" (*jāyate*). In the base-index used by this project they are 3.25 and 4.44; numbering varies by edition.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.5 (the Recipe — the birth-family rerun paragraph, Figures 19.4–19.5).

Entry data in the project's base-index: 03.0025 — जनँ, gaṇa 3, जनने, "to create, to procreate, to make"; 04.0044 — जनीँ, gaṇa 4, प्रादुर्भावे, "to be born, to become, to come into existence." The machine-readable inventory in `analysis/dhatupatha/data/dhatupatha.csv` preserves both. Traditional commentary independently confirms the distinction: the Kāśikā on 7.3.79 lists *jannī prādurbhāve* and *jana janane* as two entries. Printed and digital editions use different running numbers, so 3.25 and 4.44 identify this project's base-index rather than a universal citation scheme. The near-orbit śabdas include *janma* (birth), *jana* (people), *jananī* (mother), *janaka* (begetter), *jāta* (born), *jāti* (birth, kind), and *prajā* (offspring or subjects). The earlier list of modern-language spellings was removed because the chapter does not depend on it.

### `chambers-1872-king-kin`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- chambers-etymological-dictionary-1872-scan | title page and pp. 281–282
-->

**Short:** James Donald, ed., *Chambers's Etymological Dictionary of the English Language* (Edinburgh: W. & R. Chambers, 1872), pp. 281–82, ends the entries for *kin* and *king* at real, unstarred Sanskrit forms. **KIN** is "akin to *jan*, to beget, root of *Genus*"; **KING** is "lit. the father of a people" and cites "Sans. *ganaka*, father—root *gan*, to beget." Nineteenth-century works used *gan / jan* for the same Sanskrit family.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.6 (the dictionary-shift section — the "honest shelf," real unstarred Sanskrit in the chain) and the chapter opening.

Verified against the printed page-images of the 1872 edition (compiled by James Donald; preface dated August 1867). The KIN entry appears on p. 281: *"Kin, kin, n., offspring, persons of the same family; relatives; relationship; affinity… [A.S. cyn. Ice. kyn, family, race; A.S. cennan, to beget; akin to jan, to beget, root of Genus.]"* The KING entry follows on p. 282: *"King, king, n. lit. the father of a people; the chief ruler of a nation; a monarch… [A.S. cyning—cyn, offspring; Sans. ganaka, father—root gan, to beget.] See Kin."* No asterisk or reconstructed proto-form appears in either bracket. Source: Internet Archive scan `chamberssetymolo00donarich`, title page and pp. 281–82.

### `skeat-aryan-roots-and-edition-drift`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- skeat-etymological-dictionary-1882-scan | pp. 172, 255, and 729–745
- skeat-etymological-dictionary-1910-scan | pp. 150, 287–288, and 751–758
-->

**Short:** Walter W. Skeat's *Etymological Dictionary* (1882) prints the reconstructed beget-form as unstarred **GAN**, cites Sanskrit *jan*, and arranges its "List of Aryan Roots" by the Sanskrit alphabet. The new edition prepared in 1909 and printed in 1910 retitles the appendix "List of Indogermanic Roots," changes the GENUS chain from GAN to GEN, adds reconstructed *genes-* to the Latin stem, and changes CURT from SKAR to SKER. The primary scans document the reference culture moving between editions; they do not show Skeat concealing that his capitalized roots were reconstructions.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.4 (the bakery-scaled beat — the movement of the starred reconstruction into source-position) and §19.6 (the asterisk moving from beside the real forms to above them). The "Watch the Asterisk Move" figure draws on the GENUS then/now.

**The 1882 first edition** (Internet Archive `in.ernet.dli.2015.83588`; title page "REV. WALTER W. SKEAT, M.A.", "M DCCC LXXXII"): the appendix "List of Aryan Roots" opens *"The following is a brief list of the principal Aryan roots occurring in English"* and states *"The roots are arranged according to the alphabetical order of the Sanskrit alphabet, by help of which we obtain an Aryan alphabet."* Its key: *"Forms in thick type, as AK, are Aryan; forms in parenthesis, as (AH), are Teutonic"* — the source prints the forms as bare thick-type capitals preceded by a radical sign, with no leading asterisk. Root 87 gives *"GA, to beget, produce… GAN (= KAN, to produce)… Skt. jan, to beget; Gk. γέν-ος, race… Lat. gi-gn-ere, to beget."* GENUS gives *"GENUS, breed, race, kin. (L.)… — GAN, to beget; cf. Skt. jan, to beget… Doublet, kin, q.v."*

Note on scope: Skeat's "Aryan" root-forms are themselves reconstructions (drawn from Fick), distinguished by him from the Sanskrit forms; the book does **not** claim Skeat treated Sanskrit as the parent. The load-bearing facts are (a) the 1882 roots are **unstarred** and Sanskrit-alphabet-ordered, with real Skt. *jan* cited inside the chain, and (b) the **movement across editions** toward starred reconstruction.

**The edition drift:** Skeat's 1882 title page identifies the Clarendon edition; its GENUS entry on p. 255 reads *"Lat. genus (stem gener-)... GAN, to beget; cf. Skt. jan, to beget,"* and its CURT entry on p. 172 reads *"SKAR, to shear, cut."* The appendix, pp. 729–45, is headed *"List of Aryan Roots"*; it identifies thick-type capitals as reconstructed Aryan forms and arranges them by the Sanskrit alphabet. Root 87 on p. 733 gives GAN and cites Sanskrit *jan*.

The new edition's preface is dated 30 December 1909, and the edition was printed in 1910. Its appendix is headed *"List of Indogermanic Roots."* The GENUS entry on pp. 287–88 now gives Latin *gener-* "for \*genes-," cites Brugmann, and points to GEN rather than GAN. The CURT entry on p. 150 points to SKER rather than SKAR. Its introductory apparatus replaces "original Aryan roots" with "original Indogermanic roots" and names Brugmann, Uhlenbeck, Prellwitz, and Kluge as its frequent authorities. These details were checked against the page-images in Internet Archive scans `in.ernet.dli.2015.83588` (1882) and `in.ernet.dli.2015.15880` (new and revised edition; 1910 printing). The chapter uses this as evidence of change across reference editions, not as a claim that Skeat personally represented the capitalized roots as recorded words.

### `muller-1863-janaka-king`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- muller-science-language-second-series-1864-scan | title page and pp. 193, 218, 255–256
-->

**Short:** Friedrich Max Müller's *Lectures on the Science of Language*, Second Series, was delivered in 1863 and published in 1864. Müller connects *kin*, *genus*, and *king* with real Sanskrit *janas* and *janaka* on pp. 193, 218, and 255–56. His frame is comparative-cognate: Sanskrit is a witness, not the parent. The chapter's narrower point is that his printed comparisons still terminate in real, unstarred Sanskrit forms rather than a starred source-form.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.6 (the "honest shelf" — even Müller, who advanced the racial frame, pointed to real unstarred Sanskrit *janas* / *janaka*, not a starred non-word).

The title page states that the lectures were delivered in February–May 1863 and identifies the publication as the Second Series, London: Longman, Green, Longman, Roberts & Green, 1864. The passages were checked against the page-images. Page 193 reads: *"Thus jan, which means to create, to produce, and which we find in Sk. janas, Gr. génos, genus, kin, is raised to jnâ…"* Page 218 reads: *"The English kin is Gothic kuni, O.H.G. chunni. In Greek génos, Lat. genus, Sk. janas, we have the same word… just as king… meant originally, like Sk. janaka, father."* Pages 255–56 read: *"The Teutonic nations… used the name Konig, or King, and this corresponds to the Sanskrit janaka. What did it mean? It simply meant father, the father of a family, 'the king of his own kin,' the father of a clan, the father of a people."*

The comparative-frame caveat is load-bearing: Müller says *"the same word"* and *"like Sk. janaka,"* treating Sanskrit as a witness to a shared form rather than the ancestor. He is cited here as a hostile witness on that narrow point. His independent rendering of *king* as *"the father of a people"* matches Chambers's *ganaka, father* (see `chambers-1872-king-kin`). Source: Internet Archive scan `s2lecturesonscie02mluoft`, title page and pp. 193, 218, 255–56.

### `rv-1-174-1-indra-asura`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- ut-rigveda-metrically-restored | RV 1.174.1
- jamison-brereton-rigveda-2014-dcs | RV 1.174.1
- wisdomlib-rigveda-asura-praised | RV 1.174.1
-->

**Short:** Ṛgveda 1.174.1 addresses Indra as *asura* in the vocative while asking him to protect the people and describing him as **सत्पति (*satpati*)**, **सत्य (*satya*)**, and the giver of strength. The occurrence is praised; the verse does not determine whether the word should be divided as ***asu-ra*** or ***a-sura***.

**Deployments:** Chapter 3 §3.6 — a praised use showing that the word *asura* does not by itself identify an antagonist.

**Saṃhitā-pāṭha:**

> त्वं राजेन्द्र ये च देवा रक्षा नॄन्पाह्यसुर त्वमस्मान् ।
> त्वं सत्पतिर्मघवा नस्तरुत्रस्त्वं सत्यो वसवानः सहोदाः ॥

**Padaccheda:** त्वम् । राजा । इन्द्र । ये । च । देवाः । रक्ष । नॄन् । पाहि । असुर । त्वम् । अस्मान् । त्वम् । सत्ऽपतिः । मघऽवा । नः । तरुत्रः । त्वम् । सत्यः । वसवानः । सहःऽदाः ॥

**Working translation, checked against Wilson and Jamison-Brereton:** "You are king, Indra. Guard the *devāḥ* and the foremost people; protect us, O *asura*. You are **सत्पति (*satpati*)**, our surpassing benefactor. You are **सत्य (*satya*)**, rich in goods and the giver of victorious strength."

Here *asura* is a vocative epithet of Indra. The same mantra also calls him **सत्पति (*satpati*)** and **सत्य (*satya*)**. It therefore establishes that *asura* can occur in praise of an actor aligned with **सत् (*sat*)** without deciding the word's division. The first line is also used as an example in the Ṛgveda-Prātiśākhya. Sources: the University of Texas metrically restored text; Jamison and Brereton's translation at RV 1.174.1; and the Wilson/Sāyaṇa presentation at [Wisdomlib](https://www.wisdomlib.org/hinduism/book/rig-veda-english-translation/d/doc830892.html).

---

### `rv-1-24-14-varuna-asura`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- ut-rigveda-metrically-restored | RV 1.24.14
- jamison-brereton-rigveda-2014-dcs | RV 1.24.14
- wisdomlib-rigveda-asura-praised | RV 1.24.14
-->

**Short:** Ṛgveda 1.24.14 addresses Varuṇa as ***asura pracetā***, an attentive or discerning *asura*, while asking him to loosen the wrongs the speakers have committed. The praised occurrence directs attention to Varuṇa's action without assigning a lexical division to *asura*.

**Deployments:** Chapter 3 §3.6 — a praised occurrence in which Varuṇa is addressed as *asura* while releasing what has bound the speaker. The action supports the chapter's release-against-withholding distinction without assigning a lexical division to this occurrence.

**Saṃhitā-pāṭha:**

> अव ते हेळो वरुण नमोभिरव यज्ञेभिरीमहे हविर्भिः ।
> क्षयन्नस्मभ्यमसुर प्रचेता राजन्नेनांसि शिश्रथः कृतानि ॥

**Padaccheda:** अव । ते । हेळः । वरुण । नमोभिः । अव । यज्ञेभिः । ईमहे । हविर्भिः । क्षयन् । अस्मभ्यम् । असुर । प्रऽचेतः । राजन् । एनांसि । शिश्रथः । कृतानि ॥

**Working translation, checked against Wilson and Jamison-Brereton:** "We seek to quiet your anger through reverence, sacrifices, and offerings, Varuṇa. Holding sway for us, attentive *asura* and king, loosen the wrongs we have committed."

Here *asura* and *rājan* are vocatives applied to Varuṇa. The verb **शिश्रथः (*śiśrathaḥ*)** asks him to loosen what the speakers have done wrong. Sources: the University of Texas metrically restored text; Jamison and Brereton's translation at RV 1.24.14; and the Wilson/Sāyaṇa presentation at [Wisdomlib](https://www.wisdomlib.org/hinduism/book/rig-veda-english-translation/d/doc829155.html).

### `rv-agni-mitra-rudra-asura`

**Short:** The Ṛgveda applies *asura* in praised contexts to Agni, Rudra, and Mitra as well as to Indra and Varuṇa. These passages establish the word's positive range without deciding its derivation occurrence by occurrence.

**Deployments:** Parked — no current manuscript deployment.

**Status:** Parked — retained as supporting evidence for the praised Rigvedic range; no current body deployment.

**RV 2.1.6 — Agni as Rudra:**

> त्वमग्ने रुद्रो असुरो महो दिवस्त्वं शर्धो मारुतं पृक्ष ईशिषे ।
> त्वं वातैररुणैर्यासि शंगयस्त्वं पूषा विधतः पासि नु त्मना ॥

"You, Agni, are Rudra, the *asura* of the great heaven; you rule (*īśiṣe*) the Marut host and the nourishing vigour (*pṛkṣ*); you travel with the ruddy winds, blessing the household; as Pūṣan you protect the worshipper by your own self." The verse places the title in a praised context of vigour and protection. It does not divide the word.

**RV 7.65.2 — Mitra-Varuṇa:** *tā́ vāṃ … devā́nām asurā́* — "you two are the *asuras* among the *devāḥ*"; the pair is asked to make the dwellings *ūrjayantīḥ*, "full of vigour." This is another praised use of the same transmitted sound-form. (Padapāṭha and Wilson/Sāyaṇa rendering at wisdomlib; cf. the per-deity survey in `working/40_reference/research/as_asura_sura_asurya_vedic_survey.md` §3.2, §3.5.)
