### `shiksha-texts-standard-list`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- shiksha-samgraha-tripathi | collected texts and editorial introduction
- pillai-apisaliya-shiksha-1971 | direct edition of Āpiśalīyaśikṣā
- bharadvaja-shiksha-edition | direct edition of Bhāradvāja Śikṣā
-->

**Short:** The five works named in Chapter 15 are received *Śikṣā* texts or text-attributions represented in published collections and editions. They do not all perform the same task: some specify sound production and recitation, while the ***Vāsiṣṭhī-Śikṣā*** counts the verses and prose formulas in the Śukla Yajurveda Saṃhitā as a check on the corpus.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.1 ¶ — the citation anchor for the standard list of *Śikṣā* texts.

Rāma Prasāda Tripāṭhī's *Śikṣā Saṃgraha* includes the ***Yājñavalkya, Vāsiṣṭhī,*** and ***Pāṇinīya Śikṣās*** among a much larger collection. Its introduction describes the ***Yājñavalkya-Śikṣā*** as treating sound classes, articulatory places, duration, and related recitational distinctions. It describes the ***Vāsiṣṭhī-Śikṣā*** more narrowly: the text counts the *ṛcs* and *yajus* in each chapter of the Śukla Yajurveda Saṃhitā so that additions or losses can be detected. The older endnote incorrectly called this a Sāmavedic recitation manual.

Published direct editions also establish the ***Āpiśalīyaśikṣā*** and ***Bhāradvāja Śikṣā*** as received texts. The attribution of the former to Āpiśali does not by itself date the surviving composition to Āpiśali's lifetime. Chapter 15 therefore uses these titles to show the breadth of the *Śikṣā* literature without assigning one date or one identical function to all of them.

Sources: Rāma Prasāda Tripāṭhī, ed., *Śikṣā Saṃgraha of Yājñavalkya and Others*, introduction and contents; K. Raghavan Pillai, ed., “Āpiśalīyaśikṣā,” *Journal of the Kerala University Oriental Research Institute and Manuscripts Library* 18.2–3 (1971): 199–204; *Bhāradvāja Śikṣā*, ed. Rāmachandra Dikshitar, V. R. Sundaram Ayyar, and P. S. BORI edition.

---

### `shiksha-first-vedanga-priority`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- sanskritdocuments-mundaka | Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad 1.1.5
-->

**Short:** ***Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad*** 1.1.5 directly lists ***Śikṣā*** first among the six Vedāṅgas: “Śikṣā, Kalpa, Vyākaraṇa, Nirukta, Chandas, and Jyotiṣa.” Other presentations may arrange the auxiliaries differently. The practical priority Chapter 15 draws from this placement is the book's architectural inference.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.1 ¶ — the citation anchor for *Śikṣā* as the first-listed *Vedāṅga*.

The direct source is ***Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad*** 1.1.5:

> **शिक्षा कल्पो व्याकरणं निरुक्तं छन्दो ज्योतिषमिति**

The verse establishes that this enumeration begins with ***Śikṣā***. It does not state why. Chapter 15 supplies the architectural explanation: Vedic preservation depends first on a trained body reproducing a sound that another trained ear can recognize. Meter, grammatical analysis, interpretation, ritual procedure, and calendrical calculation then work with the recited material.

Source: *Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad* 1.1.5.

---

### `eleven-pathas-full-list`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- iyer-vikrtis-vedic-recitation-1978 | opening classification and descriptions
-->

**Short:** The traditional division is three *prakṛti-pāṭhāḥ* — *Saṃhitā, Pada,* and *Krama* — and eight *vikṛti-pāṭhāḥ* — *Jaṭā, Mālā, Śikhā, Rekhā, Dhvaja, Daṇḍa, Ratha,* and *Ghana*.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.2 ¶ — the citation anchor for the full list of the eleven *pāṭhas* with their permutational specifications.

See endnote `eleven-pathas` for the full enumeration, the source of the three/eight classification, and the limits of the error-detection analogy. Chapter 15 §§15.2–15.3 explains how the recitation modes create overlapping checks.

Standard references and full discussion: see endnote `eleven-pathas`.

---

### `ghanapathi-title-recognition`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- staal-nambudiri-veda-recitation-1961 | §3, title recorded as ghanapathikal
-->

**Short:** ***Ghanapāṭhī*** denotes a reciter who has mastered the ***Ghana-pāṭha***. Frits Staal records the corresponding title ***ghanapathikal*** in South Indian Vedic practice and states that mastery of *ghana* presupposes mastery of the preceding recitation forms used there.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.2 ¶ — the citation anchor for the *Ghanapāṭhī* honorific as social recognition of mastery of the *Ghana-pāṭha*.

Staal's transcription of the living recitation systems lists *krama, jaṭā,* and *ghana* and then states: “Whoever has mastered the last, and hence also the previous vikṛtis, is called ‘ghanapathikal’.” This evidence establishes the title and the depth of training it signifies in the community he documented. It does not establish the older note's broader claims about how every Vedic community confers the title or about a formal cross-lineage authority.

Source: Frits Staal, *Nambudiri Veda Recitation* (Mouton, 1961), §3, “Vikṛtis of the Tamil Ṛgveda and Yajurveda Recitation.”

---

### `six-vikrti-pathas-pattern-list`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- iyer-vikrtis-vedic-recitation-1978 | descriptions of all eight vikṛtis
-->

**Short:** The traditional list contains eight, not six, ***vikṛti-pāṭhāḥ***: ***Jaṭā, Mālā, Śikhā, Rekhā, Dhvaja, Daṇḍa, Ratha,*** and ***Ghana***. This endnote identifier is retained only so existing manuscript markers remain stable.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.2 ¶ — the citation anchor for the eight *vikṛti-pāṭhas* and their permutational patterns.

Iyer quotes the traditional enumeration from *Vikṛtivallī* 5 and then describes the patterns. ***Jaṭā*** repeats each pair forward, backward, and forward. ***Mālā*** alternates units moving from the beginning and end. ***Śikhā*** adds the next word to each *jaṭā* unit. ***Rekhā*** builds progressively longer units and reverses each one. ***Dhvaja*** combines units from opposite ends while retaining the internal word order. ***Daṇḍa*** progressively enlarges the unit while retaining its first word. ***Ratha*** combines equal groups of words in several arrangements. ***Ghana*** extends the *jaṭā* pattern by adding the next word before reversing and returning.

Source: S. Venkitasubramonia Iyer, “Vikṛtis in Vedic Recitation,” in *Kṛtyaratnāvalī* (Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute, 1978), pp. 1–7 of the article.

---

### `crc-error-detection-analogy`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- nist-crc-glossary | official definition of CRC as a data-integrity check
- nist-crc-dads | recalculation and comparison at the receiver
-->

**Short:** A cyclic redundancy check derives a compact check value from transmitted data. The receiver calculates the value again from the data that arrived and compares the result with the transmitted value. A mismatch detects an accidental change without requiring the system to interpret the content.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.3 opening — the computing analogy used to explain how the Vedic preservation architecture detects deviation in a recited sound-stream.

The US National Institute of Standards and Technology defines CRC as a checksum algorithm used to provide data integrity where accidental changes are expected, and as a method for determining whether data changed while passing through a communication channel. NIST's *Dictionary of Algorithms and Data Structures* further explains that the CRC can be recalculated and compared with the transmitted CRC to detect many transmission errors.

Sources: NIST Computer Security Resource Center, [“cyclic redundancy check (CRC)”](https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/cyclic_redundancy_check); Paul E. Black, [“cyclic redundancy check,”](https://xlinux.nist.gov/dads/HTML/cyclicRedundancyCheck.html) *NIST Dictionary of Algorithms and Data Structures*, revised July 19, 2021.

---

### `combinatorial-redundancy-comparative`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- iyer-vikrtis-vedic-recitation-1978 | specified combinations and reversals
- staal-nambudiri-veda-recitation-1961 | recorded krama, jaṭā, and ghana patterns
-->

**Short:** The *pāṭhas* preserve several known relationships among the same words: connected junctions, separated word forms, overlapping pairs, reversals, and longer moving combinations. A changed word or join can therefore disturb more than one relationship. Chapter 15 calls this combinatorial redundancy.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.3 ¶ — the citation anchor for the internal combinatorial operation of the Vedic *pāṭhas*.

Iyer's descriptions establish the specified patterns. Staal records *krama* as overlapping pairs, *jaṭā* as repeated forward and reverse pairs, and *ghana* as a longer sequence that advances through the passage. The resulting error-detection claim is an engineering inference: if a reciter changes one item, the change must remain compatible with several separately specified arrangements and with the listeners who know them.

The broader comparison with Hebrew, Quranic, and Latin preservation belongs to Chapter 15 §15.5 and its dedicated endnotes. It is not needed to establish the internal operation described at this marker.

Sources: S. Venkitasubramonia Iyer, “Vikṛtis in Vedic Recitation,” in *Kṛtyaratnāvalī* (Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute, 1978); Frits Staal, *Nambudiri Veda Recitation* (Mouton, 1961), §3.

---

### `nambudiri-vedic-recitation-isolation`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- gray-nambudiri-recitation-1959 | pp. 499–530; 1957 recordings
- neelakandhan-veda-kerala | Kerala branch-specific traditions
- ignca-oral-tradition-vedas | catalogue pp. 1–2
-->

**Short:** Kerala preserves distinct Nambūdiri Vedic traditions through local teaching institutions and teacher-student transmission. Recordings made there in 1957 allowed J. E. B. Gray to publish a detailed analysis of Nambūdiri Ṛgvedic *saṃhitā* recitation and accent. A Ministry of Culture survey prepared for UNESCO separately documents living Vedic traditions in Kerala, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu, Maharashtra, and Odisha.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.4 — the geographic distribution of living Vedic traditions and the 1957 Nambūdiri recordings.

The Government of India's Vedic Heritage Portal describes the Kerala Ṛgvedic tradition as taught orally in homes and residential institutions. Students memorize the *Saṃhitā* and then learn additional recitation forms and preservation practices. The same survey distinguishes Nambūdiri and Tamil Jaiminīya Sāmaveda traditions within Kerala, demonstrating that even one region can carry more than one specified lineage.

The recording evidence is exact. J. F. Staal recorded Nambūdiri Vedic recitation in Kerala in 1957. Gray used those recordings for his 1959 analysis of Ṛgvedic *saṃhitā* recitation and Vedic accent; copies were deposited at Leiden and Utrecht. Gray's study documents the Nambūdiri recitation in detail. It does **not** by itself establish agreement with every other Vedic lineage, so this note does not use it for that broader claim.

Sources: J. E. B. Gray, [“An Analysis of Nambudiri Ṛgvedic Recitation and the Nature of the Vedic Accent”](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0041977X00065551), *Bulletin of the School of Oriental and African Studies* 22, no. 3 (1959): 499–530; C. M. Neelakandhan, [“Oral and Textual Traditions of Veda in Kerala”](https://vedicheritage.gov.in/pdf/Oral_Textual_Traditions_Veda_Kerala.pdf), Vedic Heritage Portal; Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts, [*Oral Tradition of Vedas*](https://ignca.gov.in/wp-content/uploads/2015/10/media_centre_dvds.pdf), produced for the Ministry of Culture's UNESCO submission, pp. 1–2 of the catalogue.

---

### `staal-agni-nambudiri-recording`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- staal-agni-fire-altar-1983 | vol. I title page, contents, and preface p. xxi
-->

**Short:** A Nambūdiri ***Agnicayana*** was performed in Kerala from 12 to 24 April 1975 so that it could be observed, filmed, recorded, photographed, and documented. Frits Staal and his collaborators published the resulting study as the two-volume *Agni: The Vedic Ritual of the Fire Altar* in 1983.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.4 ¶ — the citation anchor for the Frits Staal 1975 Nambūdiri *Agnicayana* recording expedition.

Staal's preface states that he urged the Nambūdiris to perform the twelve-day Atirātra-Agnicayana so that it could be filmed and recorded. They agreed after discussions about attendance and costs. The book gives the performance dates as 12–24 April 1975. Its title page credits Adelaide deMenil's photographs, and its contents document the performance day by day.

The evidence establishes extensive documentation of one Nambūdiri Vedic practice. It does not by itself establish agreement among different Vedic lineages, and the older note's unsupported list of archive locations has been removed.

Source: Frits Staal, with C. V. Somayajipad and M. Itti Ravi Nambudiri, *Agni: The Vedic Ritual of the Fire Altar*, 2 vols. (Asian Humanities Press, 1983), vol. I, title page, contents, and preface p. xxi.

### `cross-shakha-verification-fieldwork`

**Short:** Government and scholarly documentation records distinct Vedic recitation traditions in several regions of the Indian subcontinent. The sources identify the branch, location, teaching method, and recitation form. They establish distributed preservation and bounded lineage differences; they do not justify a claim that every recorded performance has been compared sound by sound with every other lineage.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.4 ¶ — the citation anchor for the cross-*śākhā* verification fieldwork on Vedic recitation.

The Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts documented Jaiminīya and Rāṇāyanīya Sāmaveda traditions and Śaunaka and Paippalāda Atharvaveda traditions in Kerala, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu, Maharashtra, and Odisha. Its catalogue also records the use of जटा (*jaṭā*) and घन (*ghana*) recitation and describes the surviving schools as geographically isolated. The Kerala survey on the Vedic Heritage Portal adds branch-specific detail: Nambūdiri and Tamil Jaiminīya traditions coexist in Kerala, and their texts and chanting practices contain identifiable differences.

These sources support the claim made in Chapter 15: separate communities preserve named branches through specified methods. They do not support the earlier, stronger claim that a single fieldwork program demonstrated phoneme-by-phoneme agreement across every branch. That claim has been removed.

Sources: Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts, [*Oral Tradition of Vedas*](https://ignca.gov.in/wp-content/uploads/2015/10/media_centre_dvds.pdf), pp. 1–2 of the catalogue; C. M. Neelakandhan, [“Oral and Textual Traditions of Veda in Kerala”](https://vedicheritage.gov.in/pdf/Oral_Textual_Traditions_Veda_Kerala.pdf); Wayne Howard, *Sāmavedic Chant* (Yale University Press, 1977), for detailed analysis of Sāmavedic transmission.

---

### `masoretic-codification-timing`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- aleppo-codex-masorah | consonantal text, vocalization, accents, and Masoretic notes
-->

**Short:** The Masoretic apparatus joins an inherited consonantal text with vowel signs, cantillation marks, and textual notes. Earlier manuscript evidence contains both forms close to the later Masoretic text and other textual forms; it does not support the older note's claim that one consonantal text was already fixed in its present form before the Masoretes.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.5 ¶ — the citation anchor for the chronology of the Masoretic codification relative to the underlying consonantal text.

The Aleppo Codex project describes four features together: the consonantal text, vocalization, accents, and Masoretic notes. The mature Tiberian apparatus is represented by manuscripts such as the Aleppo Codex. Earlier witnesses show a history of textual variation as well as increasing stabilization. Chapter 15 therefore uses the narrow comparison it needs: the Masoretic apparatus preserves a written Hebrew text through visible textual and recitational notation. It does not claim that this apparatus was simply added to a single already uniform consonantal text.

Source: The Aleppo Codex project, “The Masoretes and the Masorah,” §§2.1–2.5. See also endnote `masoretic-engineered-preservation`.

---

### `quran-recitation-vs-pathas-comparison`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- nasser-quran-canonizations | seven- and ten-reading canonizations
- iyer-vikrtis-vedic-recitation-1978 | Vedic recitation classification and patterns
-->

**Short:** Quranic ***qirāʾāt*** preserve named canonical readings that contain documented differences. The Vedic *pāṭhas* instead rearrange and analyze the words of one received recitation through specified patterns. The two systems therefore preserve through different operations.

**Deployments:** Chapter 15 §15.5 ¶ — the citation anchor for the comparative analysis between Quranic recitation traditions and the eleven Vedic *pāṭhas*.

The Quranic system and the Vedic system both rely on trained recitation and teaching chains. Their internal structures differ. The canonical ***qirāʾāt*** contain named differences in pronunciation and, in some cases, wording. The Vedic *pāṭhas* do not constitute eleven variant Vedas. The three *prakṛti* modes and eight *vikṛti* modes preserve, separate, overlap, reverse, and recombine the words of the received Vedic passage according to specified rules.

Chapter 15 compares those operations. Its CRC language remains an analogy: the alternate Vedic arrangements provide redundant relationships that can expose a departure. The sources do not assign a numerical error-correction depth to either tradition, so this note does not rank them by an invented metric.

Sources: Shady H. Nasser, “The Canonizations of the Qurʾān: Political Decrees or Community Practices?”; S. Venkitasubramonia Iyer, “Vikṛtis in Vedic Recitation,” in *Kṛtyaratnāvalī* (Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute, 1978). See also endnotes `quranic-engineered-preservation` and `eleven-pathas`.

---

### `conlangs-tolkien-okrand`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- tolkien-estate-invented-languages | Tolkien's invented languages
- klingon-language-institute-new-words | creator-authorized lexical additions
- dothraki-creator-site | creation and continuing vocabulary expansion
- orwell-newspeak-appendix | deliberate contraction of Newspeak vocabulary
-->

**Short:** Quenya, Sindarin, Klingon, Dothraki, and Newspeak are openly presented as deliberately created languages. They all possess grammatical operations. Chapter 2 places them in its low-generativity quadrant because their reusable vocabularies remain bounded by an author, an authorized corpus, or creator-approved additions rather than by the absence of grammar.

**Deployments:** Chapter 2 §2.3 — examples of deliberately constructed languages; Chapter 19 §19.1 — the citation anchor for the modern constructed-language (*conlang*) tradition's honest self-presentation as invented-from-scratch artificial languages.

Tolkien described himself as having made imaginary languages since childhood and called the principal languages of his legendarium his own construction. The Tolkien Estate's account traces the changing phonology, grammar, and vocabulary of Quenya and Sindarin across his manuscripts. The languages were never presented as the recovered speech of a historical community.

Marc Okrand created Klingon for *Star Trek*. The Klingon Language Institute's list of later words records the source of each addition and distinguishes words supplied by Okrand from other material. David J. Peterson's Dothraki site likewise identifies him as the language's creator and describes a vocabulary that continues to grow. In both cases, the creator or an authorized source still determines which additions enter the canonical language.

Newspeak is a deliberately restrictive case. Orwell's appendix to *Nineteen Eighty-Four* states that it was designed to reduce the range of thought and that its vocabulary would shrink rather than expand. It belongs in the same quadrant for a different reason: restriction was its stated purpose.

The chapter's term *low generativity* does not mean that these languages lack morphology, syntax, or the ability to form sentences. It identifies their dependence on a bounded lexicon whose canonical extension still comes from an author, fictional corpus, or authorized source. That is the book's analytical category rather than a standard classification used by conlang creators.

Sources: Carl F. Hostetter, [“Tolkien's Invented Languages,” Tolkien Estate](https://www.tolkienestate.com/scholarship/carl-hostetter-tolkiens-invented-languages/); Klingon Language Institute, [“New Klingon Words”](https://www.kli.org/about-klingon/new-klingon-words/all/); David J. Peterson, [“About Dothraki”](https://dothraki.com/about-dothraki/); George Orwell, [“The Principles of Newspeak,” appendix to *Nineteen Eighty-Four*](https://orwell.ru/library/novels/1984/english/en_app).

---

### `pie-term-history`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- schleicher-compendium-1874-english | nineteenth-century English terminology
- etymonline-proto-indo-european | adjective recorded by 1905
- lehmann-pie-phonology-1952 | term in title and abbreviation in scholarly use
-->

**Short:** The reconstructed object is older than its current English name. Schleicher's 1861 *Compendium* and 1868 fable used *indogermanisch* and *Ursprache*. The adjective *Proto-Indo-European* is recorded by 1905. Winfred P. Lehmann's *Proto-Indo-European Phonology* shows that both the full term and the abbreviation *PIE* were established scholarly language by 1952. The currently available sources do not establish a precise first appearance or the decade in which the abbreviation became routine.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.4 ¶ — the citation anchor for the historical chronology of *Proto-Indo-European* as a stable term.

The term ***Proto-Indo-European*** (PIE) enters the academic literature as a stable terminological-conceptual reference relatively recently in the history of comparative philology:

- **Mid-nineteenth century**: Schleicher's *Compendium* (1861) and his fable (1868) use *indogermanisch* (Indo-Germanic) as the family-name and refer to the reconstructed common ancestor as the *Ursprache* (proto-language) or *indogermanische Ursprache*. The term *Proto-Indo-European* itself does not appear in this form.

- **Late nineteenth century**: The neogrammarian period (Brugmann's *Grundriss*, 1886–1893; the Karl Verner contributions; the broader Junggrammatiker school) consolidates the reconstruction methodology. The terminology stabilizes around *indogermanisch* in German scholarship and *Indo-European* in English-language scholarship; the proto-language is referred to as the *Ursprache* or *the parent language* or *common Indo-European*.

- **Early twentieth century**: The adjective *Proto-Indo-European* is recorded by 1905. That date establishes use by that year; it does not establish the first occurrence or prove that the expression was already the field's stable default.

- **Mid-twentieth century**: Winfred P. Lehmann's *Proto-Indo-European Phonology* (1952) uses the full term in its title and *PIE* throughout the work. Pokorny's *Indogermanisches etymologisches Wörterbuch* (1959) continued to use the German abbreviation *idg.*. These records establish concurrent usage; they do not by themselves date when *PIE* became routine across the whole field.

- **Late twentieth century onward**: *PIE* becomes ubiquitous in routine academic and reference usage. The Online Etymology Dictionary (etymonline.com, launched 2001), Wiktionary, and the broader online etymological-reference ecosystem standardize on *PIE* as the routine terminological reference.

The sources securely establish a change in terminology: Schleicher wrote of an Indo-European or Indo-Germanic original language, while twentieth-century English scholarship standardized the present name and abbreviation. A precise claim about when the term became stable or routine requires a dedicated bibliographic corpus search. See endnote `pie-cementing-recent-decades` for the separate growth of PIE notation in recent dictionaries and online references.

Sources: August Schleicher, *A Compendium of the Comparative Grammar of the Indo-European, Sanskrit, Greek and Latin Languages*, trans. Herbert Bendall (London: Trübner, 1874), printed pp. 5 and 8; [Online Etymology Dictionary, “Proto-Indo-European”](https://www.etymonline.com/word/Proto-Indo-European), for use by 1905; Winfred P. Lehmann, [*Proto-Indo-European Phonology*](https://lrc.la.utexas.edu/books/piep/index) (University of Texas Press and Linguistic Society of America, 1952), title and passim.

---

### `thomason-kaufman-1988`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- thomason-kaufman-1988 | pp. 14 and 74–76
- ucpress-thomason-kaufman | publication record
-->

**Short:** Sarah Grey Thomason and Terrence Kaufman, *Language Contact, Creolization, and Genetic Linguistics* (University of California Press, 1988) argues that, under sufficient contact, structural as well as lexical features can transfer between languages. Its borrowing scale has **five levels**, from casual contact through very strong cultural pressure, with progressively deeper effects predicted at each level. The scale supports the possibility of extensive transfer under prolonged contact; it does not determine the historical direction of any particular Sanskrit encounter.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.7 — the citation anchor for the Thomason-Kaufman account of language contact and structural borrowing.

Sarah Grey Thomason and Terrence Kaufman, *Language Contact, Creolization, and Genetic Linguistics* (University of California Press, 1988). The book is the foundational late-twentieth-century reframing of language-contact theory in historical linguistics. The two principal claims the chapter draws on:

**First**, Thomason and Kaufman state that any linguistic feature can in principle be transferred from one language to another. Their cases demonstrate that sufficiently intense and prolonged contact can transfer grammar as well as vocabulary.

**Second**, the intensity and duration of contact predict the depth of transfer. Light contact primarily transfers vocabulary; stronger contact can also transfer phonology, morphology, and syntax. Thomason and Kaufman develop a **five-level** scale: casual contact, slightly more intense contact, more intense contact, strong cultural pressure, and very strong cultural pressure. See pp. 74–76.

The scale establishes that deep structural transfer is possible under sustained contact. It does not establish that the relevant historical contact occurred, identify its carriers, or determine its direction. Chapter 19 argues those points separately and uses the Mitanni record as one documented instance of Sanskritic specialist vocabulary within a non-Indic political setting.

Subsequent literature extending the Thomason-Kaufman account: Thomason, *Language Contact: An Introduction* (Edinburgh University Press, 2001); Yaron Matras, *Language Contact* (Cambridge University Press, 2009); Donald Winford, *An Introduction to Contact Linguistics* (Wiley-Blackwell, 2003); the *Journal of Language Contact* (Brill, 2007–present).

Standard reference: Sarah Grey Thomason and Terrence Kaufman, *Language Contact, Creolization, and Genetic Linguistics* (University of California Press, 1988), especially p. 14 and pp. 74–76.

---

### `ross-metatypy-takia`

**Short:** Malcolm Ross, "Contact-Induced Change and the Comparative Method: Cases from Papua New Guinea," in Durie & Ross, eds., *The Comparative Method Reviewed* (Oxford University Press, 1996): 180–217 — develops the concept of ***metatypy***, in which sustained contact restructures one language's grammar toward another language while much of its inherited vocabulary remains. Ross's standard case compares Takia with neighboring Waskia in Papua New Guinea. The concept remains useful for a possible future comparison but does not describe the transfer of an analytical method examined in Chapter 20.

**Status:** Parked — retained as a possible comparison for contact-induced structural transfer. The Chapter 20 deployment was removed because the chapter concerns the transfer of an analytical method, while *metatypy* describes the restructuring of a language through sustained contact.

**Deployments:** Retained as source material for future use.

Malcolm Ross developed the concept of ***metatypy*** in his 1996 paper "Contact-Induced Change and the Comparative Method: Cases from Papua New Guinea" (in *The Comparative Method Reviewed*, ed. Mark Durie and Malcolm Ross, Oxford University Press, 1996). The concept captures the most extreme outcome of contact-induced structural change: *a language's morphosyntax is wholesale restructured to match a model language, while the recipient retains its inherited vocabulary*.

The mechanism Ross developed: under conditions of prolonged bilingualism in a community with strong social/cultural orientation toward the *model* language, the speakers of the *replica* language progressively restructure their language's morphosyntax to match the model. The vocabulary remains inherited within the replica language; the grammar conforms increasingly closely to the model. Ross's central observation: metatypy is typically *asymmetric* — one language is the model, the other is the replica; the replica restructures itself toward the model; the model is structurally untouched.

The standard case study: ***Takia***, an Austronesian (Oceanic) language spoken on Karkar Island in Papua New Guinea, restructured by sustained contact with ***Waskia***, a Papuan (Trans-New Guinea) language spoken on the same island. Over generations of bilingualism (most Takia speakers also spoke Waskia; Waskia speakers were largely monolingual or less commonly bilingual), the Takia community restructured Takia's morphosyntax to match Waskia's grammatical organization while preserving Takia's vocabulary. The result: Takia today retains Austronesian vocabulary but Papuan grammar — a language whose typological profile is at the boundary of two major language families.

Possible future use: the Takia case provides a documented example of asymmetrical structural influence during sustained bilingual contact. It should be used only where the subject is a receiving language restructuring its grammar toward a model language. Chapter 20 instead examines communities receiving Sanskritic words or methods of analysis, so the term was removed from that chapter.

Standard references: Malcolm Ross, "Contact-Induced Change and the Comparative Method: Cases from Papua New Guinea," in Mark Durie and Malcolm Ross, eds., *The Comparative Method Reviewed: Regularity and Irregularity in Language Change* (Oxford University Press, 1996), pp. 180–217. Ross's subsequent papers extend the framework: "Metatypy" entry in the *International Encyclopedia of Linguistics* (Oxford University Press, second edition 2003); "Diagnosing Contact Processes from Their Outcomes," in *Studies in Language* 37 (2013). For the Takia/Waskia specifically: Malcolm Ross, *Takia, a Language of Karkar Island, Madang Province* (Pacific Linguistics, Australian National University, 2002).

---

### `as-bhu-being-paradigm`

**Short:** Sanskrit preserves **⟪अस्⟫ (*as*)** and **⟪भू⟫ (*bhū*)** as two atoms, uses both in Ṛgveda 10.129, and later documents their grammatical relationship through *Aṣṭādhyāyī* 2.4.52, **अस्तेर्भूः**. Latin joins their reflections inside ***sum, esse, fuī, futūrus***; Greek preserves them more visibly across **ἐστί (*esti*)** and the **φυ- (*phu-*)** family.

**Status:** Parked — intentionally undeployed; retained source material.

**Deployments:** Retained as source material for future use. The former Chapter 19 §19.9 and Appendix Part 1 §1.5 deployments are preserved in `working/40_reference/source_material/as_bhu_being_becoming_case_lost_and_found.md`.

The *Dhātupāṭha* records **असँ भुवि (*asaṃ bhuvi*)** and **भू सत्तायाम् (*bhū sattāyām*)** as separate entries. Their English translations overlap because both concern being, but their Sanskrit forms and uses remain distinct. **⟪अस्⟫** supplies forms such as *asti*, *āsīt*, *astu*, and *syāt*. **⟪भू⟫** supplies *bhavati*, *babhūva*, *bhūta*, and *bhaviṣyati*.

Ṛgveda 10.129 places both dhātavaḥ inside one sūkta. Mantra 1 has **नासदासीन्नो सदासीत्तदानीम् (*nāsad āsīn no sad āsīt tadānīm*)**, "then non-being was not, nor was being." Mantra 6 asks **अथा को वेद यत आबभूव (*athā ko veda yat ābabhūva*)**, "then who knows what came into being?" These forms establish the Vedic use before Pāṇini's documentation.

*Aṣṭādhyāyī* 2.4.52 states **अस्तेर्भूः (*aster bhūḥ*)**. The *Kāśikāvṛtti* explains that **⟪भू⟫** replaces **⟪अस्⟫** when an *ārdhadhātuka* affix follows and gives *bhavitā*, *bhavitum*, and *bhavitavyam* as examples. The *Laghusiddhāntakaumudī* adds *babhūva* and *bhaviṣyati*, while contrasting forms such as *astu* that remain with **⟪अस्⟫**.

Latin grammar presents ***sum, esse, fuī, futūrus*** as one irregular verb. Its present system uses ***s- / es-*** forms, while its perfect system and future participle use ***fu- / fut-***. *Allen and Greenough's New Latin Grammar* states the comparison plainly: the present forms correspond to Sanskrit *asmi / asti*, and the perfect and supine forms *fu- / fut-* are kindred with Greek *ephu* and English *be*. The Greek record also preserves **ἐστί (*esti*)** in the **⟪अस्⟫** family and **φύω (*phuō*)**, **ἔφυ (*ephu*)**, and **φύσις (*physis*)** in the **⟪भू⟫** family.

The pyramid's reconstruction places *\*h₁es-* above the first family and *\*bʰuH-* above the second. The retained analysis reverses that direction: Sanskrit preserves the sound-bearing atoms, their Vedic use, and their grammatical relation, while Greek and Latin preserve their vivimorphed reflections.

Sources: *Ṛgveda* 10.129.1 and 10.129.6; *Dhātupāṭha*, entries **असँ भुवि** and **भू सत्तायाम्**; *Aṣṭādhyāyī* 2.4.52 with the *Kāśikāvṛtti* and *Laghusiddhāntakaumudī*, [अस्तेर्भूः](https://sanskritdocuments.org/learning_tools/ashtadhyayi/vyakhya/2/2.4.52.htm); *Allen and Greenough's New Latin Grammar*, §170, [Sum](https://dcc.dickinson.edu/grammar/latin/sum); Dickinson College Commentaries, [Latin Core Vocabulary: *sum, esse, fuī, futūrum*](https://dcc.dickinson.edu/latin-core/svm1svm2); Herbert Weir Smyth, *A Greek Grammar for Colleges*, [Appendix: List of Verbs](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Perseus%3Atext%3A1999.04.0007%3Apart%3D5&force=y), s.v. *phuō*; Liddell and Scott, *A Greek-English Lexicon*, [φύσις](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Perseus%3Atext%3A1999.04.0057%3Aentry%3Dfu%2Fsis).

### `vyutpatti-fivefold-method`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- kashika-ashtadhyayi-6-3-109 | fivefold nirukta verse and textual location
- sanskritdocuments-dhatupatha-index | Sanskrit atom inventory used in the worked comparisons
-->

**Short:** The lineage-preserved fivefold method of *nirukta* and *vyutpatti* records four sound operations within Sanskrit word-analysis — addition, transposition, modification, and loss — together with a semantic extension grounded in the *dhātu*. Appendix Part 1 extends those categories into a repeatable student exercise: begin with the PIE image, gather the recorded forms, render their approximate sounds in Devanagari, and map consonants, vowels, and meaning separately.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.7 — the compact *yuj / yoke* demonstration; Appendix Part 1 §1.5 — the complete method and expanded comparative cases; Epilogue, "Where the Nectar Rises" — the research protocol for Greek, Latin, Persian, and other receiving languages.

The fivefold method is preserved in the *Kāśikāvṛtti* under *Aṣṭādhyāyī* 6.3.109:

> वर्णागमो वर्णविपर्ययश्च द्वौ चापरौ वर्णविकारनाशौ ।\
> धातोस्तदर्थातिशयेन योगस्तदुच्यते पञ्चविधं निरुक्तम् ॥
>
> *varṇāgamo varṇaviparyayaś ca dvau cāparau varṇavikāranāśau |*\
> *dhātos tadarthātiśayena yogas tad ucyate pañcavidhaṃ niruktam ||*

***Padaccheda:*** वर्ण-आगमः । वर्ण-विपर्ययः । च । द्वौ । च । अपरौ । वर्ण-विकार-नाशौ । धातोः । तत्-अर्थ-अतिशयेन । योगः । तत् । उच्यते । पञ्च-विधम् । निरुक्तम् ॥

***Translation:*** Addition and transposition of sounds are two operations; modification and loss are the other two. The fifth connects the resulting form to an extended meaning of the *dhātu*. This is called fivefold *nirukta*.

The five operations are: the addition of a sound (*varṇāgama*), the transposition of sounds (*varṇaviparyaya*), the modification of a sound (*varṇavikāra*), the loss of a sound (*varṇanāśa*), and an extended but intelligible semantic relation to the *dhātu*. The verse circulates widely as a statement of the traditional *nirukta* method; the securely located textual source used here is the *Kāśikāvṛtti* on 6.3.109 rather than a direct attribution to Yāska.

The lineage applies this formula to Sanskrit word-analysis. Chapter 19 openly extends its five categories into a proposed method for studying changed forms in receiving languages. The formula provides a disciplined way to record sound and semantic transformations; it does not by itself establish the historical direction of transmission. The Radiance project therefore requires repeated families and plausible routes of contact in addition to the fivefold analysis.

Chapter 19 begins the worked exercise with the pyramid's **\*yeug- / \*yug-** image and then reads Greek *zygon*, Latin *iugum*, Gothic *juk*, and English *yoke* one language at a time. Appendix Part 1 develops the full sound analysis. The approximate Devanagari renderings are pedagogical sound-maps rather than historical spellings. The comparison uses the *varṇamālā* as its instrument: Latin initial **i** in *iugum* represents **तालव्य अन्तःस्थ य (*tālavya antaḥstha ya*)**; the closing Greek and Latin **g** maps to **कण्ठ्य घोष अल्पप्राण ग (*kaṇṭhya ghoṣa alpaprāṇa ga*)**; and Gothic and English **k** maps to **कण्ठ्य अघोष अल्पप्राण क (*kaṇṭhya aghoṣa alpaprāṇa ka*)**. Greek **ζ** requires a period-specific account and is not silently equated with one Sanskrit sonomer. Greek **υ** in **ζυγόν** combines a tongue position near **तालव्य इ (*tālavya i*)** with the rounded lips associated with **ओष्ठ्य उ (*oṣṭhya u*)**. Sanskrit has no vowel at that exact articulatory coordinate, so the chapter uses **उ** only as an approximation.

The comparison then reaches **⟪युज्⟫ (*yuj*)**. The Veda supplies **युञ्जन्ति (*yuñjanti*)**, **युगा (*yugā*)**, and **युक्त (*yukta*)**, documenting **ज → ग → क (*j → g → k*)** inside one semantic family. Pāṇini later documents the two movements between grid addresses under their Sanskrit conditions through **चोः कुः (*coḥ kuḥ*, 8.2.30)** and **खरि च (*khari ca*, 8.4.55)**. The lineage's derivation of **योग (*yoga*)** from **⟪युज्⟫** also applies the specified **उ → ओ (*u → o*)** *guṇa* relation and **कुत्व (*kutva*)**, the corresponding palatal-to-velar operation.

Appendix Part 1 begins the second case with Vedic **बिभर्ति (*bibharti*)** from **⟪भृ⟫ (*bhṛ*)**. The word places unaspirated **ब (*b*)** beside aspirated **भ (*bh*)** inside one Sanskrit form. Greek *pherein*, Latin *ferre*, Old English *beran*, and English *bear* remain in the same labial region while changing voicing, aspiration, and closure. Hemacandra's *Siddha-Hema* 8.1.187 records aspirated stops moving toward **h** in Prakrit. That evidence documents a different natural outcome; it does not supply a rule for deriving the Greek **ph**, Latin **f**, or Germanic **b**. The third case returns to **⟪जन्⟫ (*jan*)** and tests the same sonomer path across Greek and Latin *gen-* and Germanic *kin, kind,* and *king*.

These worked cases also define the research burden. A proposed *vyutpatti* must identify every sound addition, transposition, modification, and loss that it uses; preserve a coherent semantic relation to the Sanskrit atom; recur across other word-families rather than rely on a single resemblance; and fit a plausible route of Sanskritic radiance. Unresolved consonants or vowels remain marked as unresolved rather than being hidden inside a general claim. The Epilogue asks Indian universities to repeat that test across the Greek and Latin lexicons and to publish the weak cases and counterexamples with the strong ones.

Sources: *Kāśikāvṛtti* on *Aṣṭādhyāyī* 6.3.109, [पृषोदरादीनि यथोपदिष्टम्](https://ashtadhyayi.github.io/suutra/6.3/6.3.109/?transliteration_target=iast); the lineage's derivation of [योग](https://ashtadhyayi.com/amara/3.3?page=kosha&search=%E0%A4%AF%E0%A5%8B%E0%A4%97); the Vedic, grammatical, and comparative sources listed under endnote `yuj-bhr-radiance-method`.

---

### `yuj-bhr-radiance-method`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- ut-rigveda-metrically-restored | RV 1.6.1, 7.87.4, 10.101.3, and 10.102.6
- ashtadhyayi-rule-cluster-digital | 6.1.4, 8.2.30, and 8.4.55
- hemacandra-prakrit-grammar-8-1-187 | aspirated stops to h between vowels
- ut-indo-european-lexicon-yuj-bhr | entries yeu-g- and bher-
-->

**Short:** Chapter 19 places the PIE yoke image and its receiving-language forms before the Sanskrit evidence, then renders each form approximately in Devanagari. Appendix Part 1 expands the comparison. The Veda supplies **युञ्जन्ति / युगा / युक्त (*yuñjanti / yugā / yukta*)** and **बिभर्ति (*bibharti*)** before Pāṇini documents the relevant Sanskrit operations. The first family displays **ज → ग → क (*j → g → k*)**; the second places **ब (*b*)** and **भ (*bh*)** inside one Vedic word.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.7; Appendix Part 1 §1.5; Epilogue, "Where the Nectar Rises."

Ṛgveda 1.6.1 uses **युञ्जन्ति (*yuñjanti*)**, "they yoke." Ṛgveda 10.101.3 uses **युगा (*yugā*)**, "the yokes," and Ṛgveda **10.102.6** uses **युक्त (*yukta*)**, "yoked" or "joined." Verse 10.102.9 instead has **युञ्जम् (*yuñjam*)**. These Vedic forms place palatal **ज (*j*)**, voiced velar **ग (*g*)**, and voiceless velar **क (*k*)** inside one semantic family. Pāṇini later documents the movements between their grid addresses through **चोः कुः (*coḥ kuḥ*, 8.2.30)** and **खरि च (*khari ca*, 8.4.55)**. His rules apply to Sanskrit under stated conditions; the chapter does not present them as rules applied inside Greek or Latin.

The comparative family includes Greek **ζυγόν (*zygon*)**, Latin ***iugum***, Gothic ***juk***, and English ***yoke***. The usual reconstruction places **\*yeug- / \*yug-** above them. The chapter presents that account first, then shows that the recorded Sanskrit atom **⟪युज्⟫**, its generated family, and the Vedic **j / g / k** forms contain the physical and semantic relationships that the starred image claims to explain.

Ṛgveda 7.87.4 uses **बिभर्ति (*bibharti*)**, "bears, carries, sustains," from **⟪भृ⟫ (*bhṛ*)**. The Vedic word contains both unaspirated **ब (*b*)** and aspirated **भ (*bh*)**. Pāṇini later documents the repeated element through **पूर्वोऽभ्यासः (*pūrvo 'bhyāsaḥ*, 6.1.4)** and the rules that specify its form. The comparative family includes Greek **φέρειν (*pherein*)**, Latin ***ferre***, Gothic ***bairan***, and English ***bear***. Hemacandra's *Siddha-Hema* 8.1.187 records aspirated stops changing under specified Prakrit conditions, with examples including *mukha → muha*, *megha → meha*, and *svabhāva → sahāva*. This provides an Indic method for describing natural sound change, not a direct derivation of the Greek or Latin forms.

The proposed project must therefore keep three levels distinct: Vedic forms establish what Sanskrit already uses; Pāṇini documents Sanskrit's controlled operations; Hemacandra records changes in Prakrit and Apabhraṃśa. The comparison with Greek, Latin, Germanic, and other receiving languages is a separate research test. Repetition across many families, semantic continuity, and historically plausible paths of Sanskritic radiance must establish the larger case.

Sources: *Ṛgveda* 1.6.1, 10.101.3, 10.102.6, and 7.87.4; *Dhātupāṭha*, **युजिँर् योगे**, **डुभृञ् धारणपोषणयोः**, and **भृञ् भरणे**; *Aṣṭādhyāyī* 8.2.30, [चोः कुः](https://ashtadhyayi.com/sutraani/8/2/30), 8.4.55, **खरि च**, and 6.1.4, **पूर्वोऽभ्यासः**; the lineage's derivation of [योग](https://ashtadhyayi.com/amara/3.3?page=kosha&search=%E0%A4%AF%E0%A5%8B%E0%A4%97); Hemacandra, *Siddha-Hema* 8.1.187, which gives **ख घ थ ध भाम्** and examples including *mukha → muha*, *megha → meha*, and *svabhāva → sahāva*; University of Texas Linguistics Research Center, Indo-European Lexicon entries for [*yeu-g-*](https://lrc.la.utexas.edu/lex/master/0785), [*bher-*](https://lrc.la.utexas.edu/lex/master/0229), and [*gen-*](https://lrc.la.utexas.edu/lex/master/0566).

### `upasarga-radiance-apa`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- ashtadhyayi-rule-1-4-59 | upasargāḥ kriyāyoge
- zanchi-multiple-preverbs-2019 | Vedic and Homeric preverbs
- lsj-apo | Greek preposition and preverb
- lewis-short-ab-abs | Latin preposition and compound element
-->

**Short:** Sanskrit **अप (*apa*)**, Greek **ἀπό (*apó*)**, and Latin ***ab / abs*** provide the pilot case for extending the Radiance Mapping Project from atoms to directional operators. The conventional comparison places reconstructed **\*h₂epo** above the three forms. The book begins instead from the recorded Sanskrit operator and tests whether the receiving languages preserve parts of its repeated behavior.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.7, “Operators Traveled with the Atoms”; Appendix Part 1 §1.6, “Operators in Motion”; Epilogue, “Where the Nectar Rises.”

The Sanskrit record includes **अपगच्छति (*apagacchati*)**, **अपनयति (*apanayati*)**, and **अपहरति (*apaharati*)**, which join **अप** with **⟪गम्⟫, ⟪नी⟫,** and **⟪हृ⟫** while retaining the direction away or off. Pāṇini 1.4.59, **उपसर्गाः क्रियायोगे (*upasargāḥ kriyāyoge*)**, documents the functional category when the listed element joins an action; the examples establish use rather than invention by Pāṇini.

The Greek and comparative claims used in the manuscript are deliberately narrow. Ancient Greek **ἀπό** functions as a preposition and preverb. Research on Homeric Greek and the Vedic corpus records both mobile and attached preverbs, as well as multiple-preverb constructions. The Latin ***ab / abs*** functions as a preposition, and ***ab-*** appears in verbal compounds. The body does not claim that these shared behaviors alone establish historical direction. The project repeats the comparison across the operator inventory, complete molecules, sound paths, meanings, and plausible routes of contact.

The conventional PIE account and the comparative preverb evidence are documented in: Chiara Zanchi, *Multiple Preverbs in Ancient Indo-European Languages: A Comparative Study on Vedic, Homeric Greek, Old Church Slavic, and Old Irish* (Narr Francke Attempto, 2019); the University of Würzburg project on Vedic local particles, [Projects — Chair of Comparative Philology](https://www.phil.uni-wuerzburg.de/en/vgsp/research/projects/); Thomas Olander, ed., *The Indo-European Language Family: A Phylogenetic Perspective* (Cambridge University Press, 2022), Sanskrit etymological material listing **\*h₂ep-ó**, Greek *apó*, and related forms; Liddell and Scott under Greek **ἀπό**; and [Lewis and Short under Latin *ab / abs*](https://atlas.perseus.tufts.edu/dictionaries/entry/urn%3Acite2%3Ascaife-viewer%3Adictionary-entries.atlas_v1%3Alat.ls.perseus-eng2-n4/). The exact Latin sound-history from **\*h₂epo** remains part of the research record rather than a settled premise of the body argument.

### `agastya-sources`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- ut-rigveda-metrically-restored | RV 1.165-191; RV 1.179 dialogue
- tamil-university-agastya-tamil-tradition | absence from Tolkāppiyam and later commentarial tradition
- epigraphia-indica-17-velvikkudi | pp. 291-309; Agastya in Pandya genealogy
- mahabharata-vana-agastya | Vana Parva Agastya cycle
-->

**Short:** Vedic and post-Vedic sources remember Agastya in the north, while later Tamil literary and inscriptional traditions place him deeply within the south. The *Ṛgveda* attributes 1.165–1.191 to the Agastya lineage; 1.179 is the dialogue of Agastya and Lopāmudrā. The *Mahābhārata* and *Rāmāyaṇa* remember his movement south. Tamil tradition attributes the lost *Agattiyam* to him, and the Velvikkudi grant places him in Pandya royal memory as the dynasty's family priest.

**Deployments:** Chapter 20 §20.1 ¶ — the citation anchor for the *Agastya* lineage and the dual northern-and-southern textual evidence for his role.

The *Ṛgveda* attribution is to the Agastya lineage, not jointly to Agastya and Lopāmudrā throughout the whole sequence. Lopāmudrā appears specifically in the dialogue hymn RV 1.179. The *Mahābhārata* develops an Agastya cycle in the *Vana Parva*, including the Vindhya episode, and the *Rāmāyaṇa* places Agastya in its southern geography.

The southern evidence belongs to later Tamil memory. Agastya is not named in the *Tolkāppiyam* or in Panamparanar's preface. Medieval commentators, however, attribute an earlier grammar called the *Agattiyam* to him and present it as a source or predecessor of the *Tolkāppiyam*. The work itself is lost. The eighth-century Velvikkudi copper plates place Agastya in the Pandya genealogy as family priest. These witnesses establish the depth of the southern tradition without turning every later attribution into a contemporary record of Agastya's life.

Chapter 20 uses the convergence as lineage memory of a carrier: Vedic tradition remembers Agastya; epic narrative remembers movement south; Tamil literary tradition remembers a teacher of Tamil; and Pandya inscriptional memory attaches him to the dynasty. The further claim that this pattern exemplifies transmission by absorption rather than replacement belongs to the book's argument.

Sources: *Ṛgveda* 1.165–1.191, especially 1.179; *Mahābhārata*, *Vana Parva*, Agastya cycle; S. V. Shanmugam, “Agastya in Tamil Grammatical Tradition,” Tamil University, especially the discussion of the *Tolkāppiyam* and its commentators; H. Krishna Sastri, “The Velvikudi Grant of Nedunjadaiyan,” *Epigraphia Indica* 17 (1923–24), pp. 291–309; Kamil Zvelebil, *The Smile of Murugan* (Brill, 1973).

---

### `mitanni-sanskritic-evidence`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- iranica-earliest-iranian-evidence | Mitanni treaty deities and Kikkuli aika-vartana
- thieme-mitanni-treaty-deities-1960 | JAOS 80.4, pp. 301-317
- mayrhofer-mitanni-names | royal and personal names
- iranica-artassumara | royal name and Indo-Aryan analysis
- oracc-mitanni-royal-names | cuneiform name records
-->

**Short:** Records from Mitanni and its Near Eastern setting preserve several kinds of Sanskritic material. The Hittite-Mitanni treaty invokes *Mitra-Varuṇa*, *Indra*, and the two *Nāsatyas*. The Kikkuli horse-training text uses terms including *aika-vartana*, “one turn.” Royal and personal names in the same documentary field have long received Indo-Aryan analyses, including Tushratta and Artashumara. The evidence dates the written reception of this material outside India; it does not date Sanskrit's formation.

**Deployments:** Chapter 20 §20.1 ¶ — the citation anchor for the Mitanni Sanskritic-linguistic evidence.

The *Mitanni* kingdom of northern Mesopotamia (modern-day eastern Turkey, northern Iraq, and northeastern Syria) — at its peak from approximately the sixteenth to fourteenth centuries BCE by conventional chronology — preserves several distinct lines of evidence for Sanskritic-linguistic content in a non-Indic political-cultural context:

**The Hittite-Mitanni treaty.** The treaty between Suppiluliuma I (Hittite king) and Shattiwaza (Mitanni king-claimant), preserved on cuneiform tablets ***CTH 51*** (= ***KBo I 1***, the principal tablet with the Hittite-perspective historical prologue) and ***CTH 52*** (the companion tablet with the Mitanni-perspective prologue) in the Bogazköy archive (the Hittite royal archive at Hattusa), invokes *Mitra*, *Varuṇa*, *Indra*, and *Nāsatya* (the *Aśvins*) as treaty-witness deities — four Vedic devas named explicitly in a non-Indic diplomatic document, in Sanskritic form. The cuneiform transliteration: *mi-it-ra*, *u-ru-wa-na* (also recorded as *a-ru-na*), *in-da-ra* (also recorded as *in-tar*), *na-ša-ti-ya-an-na* (with Hurrian plural-ending *-nna*).

**The Kikkuli horse-training treatise.** A 184-day, 1080-line manual on horse training composed on four cuneiform tablets (the *Kikkuli* tablets, catalogued as ***CTH 284, 285, 286*** in the Hittite archive; principal Late Hittite copy CTH 284, Middle Hittite copies CTH 285 and 286; the text dates from approximately the early-to-mid second millennium BCE in the standard Hittite chronology). The text uses Sanskritic numerical terms in specific contexts:

- *aika* = Sanskrit *eka* (one)
- *tera* = Sanskrit *tri* (three)
- *panza* = Sanskrit *pañca* (five)
- *satta* = Sanskrit *sapta* (seven)
- *na* = Sanskrit *nava* (nine)
- *vartana* = Sanskrit *vartana* (turn / rotation)

Sanskrit's analytical continuum derives **एक (*eka*)** internally from the atom ⟪इ⟫ — **इण् गतौ (*iṇ gatau*)** — with the Uṇādi affix **कन् (*kan*)**. *Uṇādi-sūtra* 3.43 gives the operation directly: **इण्भीकापाशल्यतिमर्चिभ्यः कन् (*iṇ-bhī-kai-pā-śal-yati-marci-bhyaḥ kan*)**. The final *n* receives the marker designation under *Aṣṭādhyāyī* 1.3.3 and disappears under 1.3.9, leaving *ka*; *guṇa* under 7.3.84 changes **इ (*i*)** to **ए (*e*)**. The result is *eka*. Within the book's architectural account, *eka* is therefore an engineered Sanskrit molecule rather than a contraction descended from Mitanni *aika*. The direction runs from Sanskrit into the receiving language: *aika* records how the Mitanni linguistic setting rendered *eka*. The tablet dates that reception; it does not date the Sanskrit formation.

**Royal and personal names.** Several names in Mitanni and neighboring records have received Indo-Aryan analyses:

- *Tushratta* → *Tveṣaratha* (*tveṣa-ratha* = brilliant-chariot).
- *Shattiwaza* → *Sātivāja* (*sāti-vāja* = victorious-power).
- *Indaruda* → *Indrota* (*indra-uta* = aided-by-Indra). Indaruda belongs to the wider Syrian-Palestinian documentary field and should not be listed as a Mitanni king.
- *Artashumara* → Mitanni Sanskritic *Artasmara* / Vedic cognate *Ṛtasmara* (*r̥ta-smara* = recalling-*r̥ta* / cosmic order).

**The *maryannu* designation.** Near Eastern records use *maryannu* for a chariot-warrior class associated with the Mitanni political world. Scholars commonly compare its first element with Vedic **मर्य (*marya*)**, “young man” or “warrior,” while recognizing that the recorded term belongs to a receiving linguistic setting.

The treaty and horse-training vocabulary are the strongest direct evidence. The onomastic and *maryannu* evidence broadens the pattern but depends upon linguistic analysis rather than an explicit statement of origin. Chapter 20 interprets the combined record as evidence of specialist transmission into a Hurrian-speaking political setting.

Standard references: Paul Thieme, "The 'Aryan' Gods of the Mitanni Treaties," *Journal of the American Oriental Society* 80.4 (1960): 301–317 — the standard settlement of the treaty-deity identifications. Manfred Mayrhofer, *Die Indo-Arier im alten Vorderasien: Mit einer analytischen Bibliographie* (Otto Harrassowitz, Wiesbaden, 1966) — the standard bibliographic-analytical reference on the Indo-Aryan content in the Near East; Mayrhofer, *Etymologisches Wörterbuch des Altindoarischen* (Carl Winter, Heidelberg, 1992–2001) for the onomastic corpus. Annelies Kammenhuber, *Die Arier im Vorderen Orient — ein Mythos?* (Carl Winter, Heidelberg, 1968) — the skeptical-argumentative companion that scholars have continued to engage. Edwin Bryant, *The Quest for the Origins of Vedic Culture: The Indo-Aryan Migration Debate* (Oxford University Press, 2001), the linguistic-evidence chapters on the Mitanni record; Edwin Bryant and Laurie Patton, eds., *The Indo-Aryan Controversy: Evidence and Inference in Indian History* (Routledge, 2005) — the standard edited volume in the debate. Frits Staal, *Discovering the Vedas* (Penguin India, 2008) for the broader contextual analysis. For the Kikkuli text: Annelies Kammenhuber, *Hippologia hethitica* (Otto Harrassowitz, Wiesbaden, 1961); Frank Starke, *Ausbildung und Training von Streitwagenpferden: Eine hippologisch orientierte Interpretation des Kikkuli-Textes* (Studien zu den Boğazköy-Texten 41, Otto Harrassowitz, 1995); Peter Raulwing, *The Kikkuli Text: Hittite Training Instructions for Chariot Horses* (open-access via LRGAF, 2009) — modern interdisciplinary survey. For the *maryannu* institutional account: Eva von Dassow, *State and Society in the Late Bronze Age: Alalaḫ under the Mittani Empire* (CDL Press, 2008). For the broader linguistic positioning: Alexander Lubotsky, "The Indo-Iranian Substratum," in C. Carpelan et al., eds., *Early Contacts between Uralic and Indo-European* (Mémoires de la Société Finno-Ougrienne, 2001).

### `behistun-inscription`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- ut-old-persian-behistun | Behistun text, grammar, and vocabulary
- iranica-bisotun-inscription | monument, languages, and publication history
- oxford-behistun-relief | inscription overview
-->

**Short:** The Old Persian text of the Behistun inscription contains forms that remain readily recognizable to a Sanskrit student: *tuvam / tvam*, *martiya / martya*, *nāma / nāma*, *kartam / kṛtam*, *abavam / abhavam*, and *brātā / bhrātā*. Old Persian is also highly inflected and marks grammatical relations through forms familiar from Sanskrit study. Chapter 20 uses these correspondences as a direct reader experiment.

**Deployments:** Chapter 20 §20.1 ¶ — the citation anchor for the Behistun inscription as the secondary empirical anchor for the Sanskritic-Persian cognate proximity.

The *Behistun Inscription* — *Bīsotūn* in modern Persian — is a multilingual rock relief carved on a cliff face at Mount Behistun in western Iran, commissioned by the Achaemenid king Darius I (reigned 522–486 BCE). The inscription is carved in three languages: *Old Persian* (the principal language), *Elamite*, and *Akkadian* (Babylonian). The text narrates Darius's accession to the throne and his suppression of rebellions across the Achaemenid Empire.

The University of Texas Old Persian lessons print the Behistun text beside a grammatical analysis. The inscription repeatedly uses forms such as *tuvam* (“you”), *martiya* (“man”), *nāma* (“name”), *kartam* (“done”), *abavam* (“I became”), and *brātā* (“brother”). A Sanskrit student can compare these directly with **त्वम् (*tvam*)**, **मर्त्य (*martya*)**, **नाम (*nāma*)**, **कृतम् (*kṛtam*)**, **अभवम् (*abhavam*)**, and **भ्राता (*bhrātā*)**. The same lessons document nominal cases, person, number, tense, mood, and flexible word order in Old Persian.

Modern Persian developed from later Iranian forms and no longer retains this whole inflectional system. The chapter's comparison therefore does not rest on a general impression that the languages sound alike. It rests on recognizable words and grammatical forms in the inscription itself. The larger claim that Sanskrit remained close to these old forms because it retained a calibrant is the architectural conclusion developed across the book.

Sources: University of Texas Linguistics Research Center, “Old Persian: excerpts from the Behistun inscription,” lessons 7–8; Rüdiger Schmitt, *The Bisitun Inscriptions of Darius the Great: Old Persian Text* (SOAS, 1991); Roland G. Kent, *Old Persian: Grammar, Texts, Lexicon*, 2nd ed. (American Oriental Society, 1953); *Encyclopaedia Iranica*, “Bīsotūn III: Darius's Inscriptions”; Oxford, “The Behistun Relief and Inscription.”

### `dionysius-thrax-techne`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- oxford-dionysius-thrax | contents, influence, no syntax, and authorship controversy
- uhlig-techne-grammatike-1883 | Greek text
- law-sluiter-dionysius-techne-1995 | specialist history
-->

**Short:** The *Téchnē Grammatikē* attributed to Dionysius Thrax is the earliest surviving systematic handbook of Greek grammar. It classifies letters, stops, accents, syllables, parts of speech, grammatical categories, and inflectional paradigms. It does not treat syntax, and modern scholarship disputes whether the whole received text was written by Dionysius himself. Its date and form still place the work after sustained Greco-Indic contact and before its large influence on Latin grammar.

**Deployments:** Chapter 20 §20.2 ¶ (Greek-grammar paragraph) — the citation anchor for Dionysius Thrax's *Téchnē Grammatikē* as the first systematic Greek grammar.

***Dionysius Thrax (Διονύσιος ὁ Θρᾷξ)*** lived approximately 170–90 BCE and belonged to the Alexandrian grammatical tradition. The brief ***Téchnē Grammatikē (Τέχνη Γραμματική — Art of Grammar)*** transmitted under his name classifies letters, stops, accents, syllables, the parts of speech and their grammatical categories, and several inflectional paradigms. It contains no treatment of syntax. The attribution of the complete received work to Dionysius has also been disputed, which is why Chapter 20 says “attributed to Dionysius Thrax.”

The dating context: Dionysius Thrax was active in Alexandria during the period of sustained Greco-Indic contact following Alexander's campaigns (334–323 BCE), the Mauryan-Seleucid exchanges (Megasthenes's residence at the Mauryan court c. 302 BCE; the Seleucid dynastic intermarriage with the Mauryans; the broader Hellenistic-Mauryan diplomatic apparatus), the Greco-Bactrian and Indo-Greek kingdoms (200 BCE onward), Ashoka's Greek-language edicts (the bilingual Aramaic-Greek edicts at Kandahar, third century BCE), the Buddhist mission to the Hellenistic world (third century BCE forward), and the well-documented intellectual circulation between Alexandria and the subcontinent.

Greek thinkers had examined language for centuries: Plato considered the status of names, Aristotle developed logical and grammatical categories, and the Stoics analyzed parts of speech. The *Téchnē* gives the earliest surviving compact handbook that gathers a substantial set of grammatical categories into one instructional sequence.

The Pāṇinian analytical apparatus was already available in the subcontinent before this Greek handbook. The centuries preceding it included Alexander's campaigns, Mauryan-Seleucid exchanges, Greco-Bactrian and Indo-Greek kingdoms, Aśoka's Greek inscriptions, and Buddhist movement through the Hellenistic world. Those routes make transmission possible. No source found for this note records a particular Sanskrit teacher or text entering the composition of the *Téchnē*. The Pāṇinian route remains this book's proposal from chronology, contact, and structural comparison.

Sources: Oxford Classical Dictionary, “Dionysius Thrax,” for contents, influence, lack of syntax, and the authorship dispute; G. Uhlig, ed., *Grammatici Graeci*, vol. 1 (Teubner, 1883); Alan Kemp, “The Tekhne Grammatike of Dionysius Thrax,” *Historiographia Linguistica* 13 (1986); Vivien Law and Ineke Sluiter, eds., *Dionysius Thrax and the Technē Grammatikē* (Nodus, 1995).

### `donatus-priscian-grammars`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- cambridge-donatus-priscian-medieval-grammar | Greek model and medieval use
- cambridge-before-modistae | Priscian's eighteen books and syntax in 17-18
- british-library-priscian-institutiones | manuscript and work description
-->

**Short:** Aelius Donatus's *Ars Minor* and *Ars Maior* became standard Latin teaching grammars. Priscian's *Institutiones Grammaticae* provided a larger eighteen-book account; books 17–18 treat syntax. Both adapted Greek grammatical terminology and categories to Latin, and both shaped medieval European grammar. The further Pāṇinian → Greek → Latin route remains conditional upon the Greek transmission proposed in the preceding note.

**Deployments:** Chapter 20 §20.2 ¶ (Latin-grammar paragraph) — the citation anchor for Donatus's *Ars Maior* and Priscian's *Institutiones Grammaticae* as the foundational Latin grammars transparently modeled on Greek grammatical methodology.

***Aelius Donatus*** (c. 320 – c. 380 CE), Roman grammarian, composed the ***Ars Maior*** (and the shorter *Ars Minor*) — the standard Latin grammars of the late Roman period. Donatus's works formalized Latin grammatical analysis on the Greek grammatical model, using Greek terminology adapted to Latin material. The eight parts of speech of the Greek tradition (the *Téchnē Grammatikē*'s apparatus) become the eight parts of speech of Donatus's Latin grammatical apparatus.

***Priscian (Priscianus Caesariensis)***, a Latin grammarian active in Constantinople in the early sixth century, composed the eighteen-book ***Institutiones Grammaticae***. The first sixteen books chiefly examine the parts of speech; books 17 and 18 treat syntax. It became a principal reference for medieval grammatical study.

Both texts are transparently modeled on Greek grammatical methodology. They use Greek terminology (often Latinized but recognizable), Greek analytical categories (the eight parts of speech, the case-system apparatus, the morphological-paradigm presentation), and Greek analytical structure (the discrete-rules-applied-to-categories format) applied to Latin material. The Greek grammatical tradition's influence is acknowledged in the texts themselves — Donatus and Priscian both cite Greek grammarians as their methodological antecedents.

The Greek-to-Latin step is documented. The earlier Pāṇinian-to-Greek step is the proposal examined in `dionysius-thrax-techne`. If that proposal is correct, Donatus and Priscian preserve the next stage of the route into medieval European grammar. This note does not treat the full chain as independently proved by the Latin evidence.

Sources: *Donati Ars Grammatica* in H. Keil, ed., *Grammatici Latini*, vol. 4 (Teubner, 1864); Priscian, *Institutiones Grammaticae*, in *Grammatici Latini*, vols. 2–3 (1855–59); Michael A. Covington, “Before the Modistae,” in *Syntactic Theory in the High Middle Ages* (Cambridge University Press), pp. 4–21; Vivien Law, *The History of Linguistics in Europe: From Plato to 1600* (Cambridge University Press, 2003).

### `thonmi-sambhota-tibetan-grammars`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- vollmann-tibetan-grammaticography-2008 | pp. 3-6; traditional account and historiographical caution
- miller-tibetan-grammatical-tradition-1976 | foundational works and later tradition
- namami-buddhist-literary-heritage | traditional mission and script account
-->

**Short:** Tibetan historical tradition says that Songtsen Gampo sent Thonmi Sambhoṭa to India to study and to create a Tibetan script and grammatical description. It credits him with the *Sum cu pa* and *Rtags kyi 'jug pa*, the two works upon which much later Tibetan grammar rests. Modern histories repeat the tradition while warning that the surviving evidence does not permit every biographical detail to be treated as independently established.

**Deployments:** Chapter 20 §20.2 ¶ (Tibetan-grammar paragraph) — the citation anchor for the traditional journey to India, the two foundational grammatical works, and the Brāhmī-derived Tibetan script.

***Thonmi Sambhoṭa*** (Thon-mi Sam-bho-ṭa, transliterated variously) is the traditionally-credited founder of Tibetan grammatical analysis and the originator of the Tibetan script. Traditional Tibetan-historiographical accounts (preserved in the standard Tibetan religious-historical chronicles — the *Bka' chems ka khol ma*, the *Mani bka' 'bum*, the broader Tibetan-language historical literature) record:

- In the seventh century CE, the Tibetan king ***Songtsen Gampo*** (Srong-btsan sgam-po, reigned c. 618–649 CE) dispatched a mission to India specifically to study Sanskrit grammatical methodology and to develop a writing system for the Tibetan language.

- Thonmi Sambhoṭa led the mission and studied in India. Later accounts differ about his teachers and other biographical details.

- On his return, Thonmi Sambhoṭa is credited with: (a) the design of the Tibetan script (modeled on Brāhmī, with adaptations for the Tibetan phonological inventory); and (b) the authorship of the two foundational Tibetan grammatical works, ***Sum cu pa (Sum-cu-pa — The Thirty Verses)*** and ***Rtags kyi 'jug pa (rTags kyi 'jug pa — The Application of Signs)***.

The two works are foundational within Tibetan grammatical tradition. They show Indic influence in their setting, categories, and compact rule form, but the sources checked for this note do not establish every older claim that they reproduce Pāṇinian abbreviations, metarules, and rule-precedence machinery.

The Tibetan script belongs to the Brāhmī-derived family and was adapted to Tibetan. Its letter order and organization retain Indic features, but Tibetan does not reproduce the complete Sanskrit *varṇamālā* without change.

The Tibetan case is unusually direct at the level of remembered transmission: the tradition itself records the journey to India and connects that journey with the script and the foundational grammars. Chapter 20 accurately presents this as a traditional account rather than as an independently documented travel record.

Sources: Ralf Vollmann, “Tibetan Indigenous Grammaticography,” in *The Emergence of Linguistic Form* 50 (2008), especially the account of the traditional mission and the caution about its historicity; Roy Andrew Miller, *Studies in the Grammatical Tradition in Tibet* (John Benjamins, 1976); Government of India, *Buddhist Literary Heritage in India*, discussion of Thonmi Sambhoṭa and the Tibetan script.

### `buddhist-asia-radiance`

<!-- SOURCE-RECORDS
- shi-buddhist-sanskrit-chinese-2015 | pp. 236–247
- duanmu-kubozono-japanese-phonology | Sanskrit origin of gojūon grid structure
- university-tokyo-gojuon-sanskrit | public explanation of row and column order
- dila-mahavyutpatti | early ninth-century translation glossary
- bronkhorst-sanskrit-southeast-asia | pp. 263–276
- unesco-ayutthaya | name and historical model
- unesco-angkor-advisory-1992 | Angkor through nokor from nagara
-->

**Short:** Buddhist translation and teaching carried Sanskritic vocabulary and sound-analysis into East Asia, where receiving languages adapted both to their own phonology and analytical needs. **ध्यान (*dhyāna*) → 禪 (*chán*) → 禅 (*zen*)** gives the clearest lexical path. Japanese *gojūon* ordering reflects the Sanskrit sound table studied through Siddham; Sanskrit and Siddham study also stimulated systematic Chinese analysis of initials, finals, and tones. In Tibet, the *Mahāvyutpatti* standardized Sanskrit-Tibetan translation terminology after Tibetan scholars had adapted Indic grammatical method. Southeast Asian examples traveled through several Sanskritic routes, including Sanskrit, Pāli, trade, teaching, monastic transmission, and courtly use; the umbrella *Sanskritic* is therefore more accurate than direct-Sanskrit attribution in every case.

**Deployments:** Chapter 19 §19.7, where the carrier model points forward to the Asian evidence; Chapter 20 §20.2, for the lexical, phonological, Tibetan-translation, and Southeast Asian evidence for receiving-language endowment.

**East Asian vocabulary.** Buddhist translation brought a large Sanskritic vocabulary into Chinese, after which Chinese forms entered Japanese, Korean, and Vietnamese. The chapter uses three compact paths:

- **ध्यान (*dhyāna*) → 禪 (*chán*) → 禅 (*zen*)**. The Chinese form represents the Sanskritic meditation term; Japanese received the character and its Chinese-derived reading.
- **क्षण (*kṣaṇa*) → 刹那 (*chànà*) → Japanese *setsuna***; **बोधिसत्त्व (*bodhisattva*) → 菩薩 (*púsà*) → Japanese *bosatsu***; and **निर्वाण (*nirvāṇa*) → 涅槃 (*nièpán*) → Japanese *nehan*** show the receiving languages shortening and phonologically reshaping imported forms.
- These are *Sanskritic pathways*, not proof that every surviving Chinese form was borrowed immediately from a Sanskrit speaker. Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit, Pāli and other Middle Indic forms, Central Asian languages, and earlier Chinese transcriptions could mediate individual stages. The argument concerns the visible direction and adaptation of the Sanskritic seed, not an unsupported claim of uniform direct borrowing.

For the broad Chinese evidence, see Xiangdong Shi, ["The Influence of Buddhist Sanskrit on Chinese"](https://doi.org/10.1093/oxfordhb/9780199856336.013.0069), in *The Oxford Handbook of Chinese Linguistics* (Oxford University Press, 2015), pp. 236–247. Shi identifies three consequences of Sanskrit-Chinese contact: extensive loans, translation-induced linguistic structures, and Sanskrit-stimulated phonological analysis. For the individual Buddhist terms and their East Asian forms, see Robert E. Buswell Jr. and Donald S. Lopez Jr., *The Princeton Dictionary of Buddhism* (Princeton University Press, 2014), entries for *dhyāna*, *chan*, *zen*, *kṣaṇa*, *bodhisattva*, and *nirvāṇa*.

**Japanese sound ordering.** The modern *gojūon* table crosses the vowel sequence *a-i-u-e-o* with consonant rows. The University of Tokyo's phonological-history account states directly that both row and column order derive from the Sanskrit alphabet table studied by Japanese Esoteric Buddhist monks through Siddham: ["Why is the order of the Japanese syllabary ‘a-i-u-e-o/a-ka-sa-ta-na?’"](https://www.u-tokyo.ac.jp/focus/en/features/z1304_00232.html). This is an ordering and analytical debt; the kana glyphs themselves developed from Chinese characters.

**Chinese sound analysis.** The chronology separates three related devices. Chinese rhyme-awareness is ancient. ***Fǎnqiè* (反切)**, documented from approximately the third century CE, indicates an unfamiliar pronunciation by combining the initial of one familiar character with the final of another. Rime dictionaries such as the *Qieyun* (601 CE) organize rhyme categories and preserve *fanqie* pronunciation explanations. Rime tables then convert the material into a system-wide matrix. Fragmentary precursors may reach the late Tang period; the oldest surviving tables belong to the later lineage represented by the *Yunjing*, whose extant editions date to 1161 and 1203. Sanskritic contact began centuries before the rime-table lineage, and intensive Sanskrit and *Siddhaṃ* study flourished during the Sui and Tang periods. No known Chinese rime table predates that contact.

Shi's Oxford Handbook chapter links Sanskrit and *Siddhaṃ* study to the emergence of **等韻學 (*děngyùnxué*)**, the systematic study represented by rime tables. The tables organize Chinese syllables by initials, finals, tones, and related phonological categories. The historical literature debates whether Indic phonetics supplied the whole design or decisively stimulated a Chinese development from earlier rhyme and *fanqie* analysis. Both accounts begin from the same chronological fact: the tabular matrix appears after sustained Sanskritic contact. The chapter therefore claims stimulus, adaptation, and a design debt rather than exact copying. See David Prager Branner, ed., [*The Chinese Rime Tables: Linguistic Philosophy and Historical-Comparative Phonology*](https://benjamins.com/catalog/cilt.271) (John Benjamins, 2006), and Pan Wenguo, *The Chinese Rhyme Tables: A New Probe into the Nature of Middle Chinese Phonology* (Routledge, 2024), especially the historical sequence from *Siddhaṃ* study through the matching of initials and rhymes to tabular classification.

**Tibetan translation discipline.** The **महाव्युत्पत्ति (*Mahāvyutpatti*)** — "The Great Etymology" — was compiled in the early ninth century as a Sanskrit-Tibetan lexicon for translating Buddhist texts into Tibetan. It contains thousands of Sanskrit terms with Tibetan equivalents and served as a terminology standard. This role must remain distinct from the earlier Tibetan grammatical works credited to Thonmi Sambhoṭa: the grammars adapted analytical method; the *Mahāvyutpatti* disciplined translation vocabulary. The Dharma Drum Institute of Liberal Arts provides the text and describes its compilation purpose in the [Mahāvyutpatti digital glossary](https://glossaries.dila.edu.tw/glossaries/MVP?locale=en).

**Southeast Asian routes.** Sanskrit and Pāli material reached Southeast Asia through overlapping movements of teachers, translators, monastic communities, merchants, court specialists, and local scholars. The resulting forms belong to the receiving languages:

- Malay and Indonesian ***bahasa*** continues Sanskrit **भाषा (*bhāṣā*)**, "speech; language." Sanskrit loans in Malay range well beyond learned terminology into court, kinship, trade, and ordinary vocabulary; see R. O. Winstedt, ["Sanskrit in Malay Literature"](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0041977X00062169), *Bulletin of the School of Oriental and African Studies* 20.1 (1957), pp. 599–600.
- Singapore's Urban Redevelopment Authority describes *Singapura* as Sanskrit "Lion City"; see its [Fort Canning historical account](https://www.ura.gov.sg/Conservation-Portal/Explore/History?bldgid=FCCSAS).
- UNESCO relates Thai *Ayutthaya* to the model of **अयोध्या (*Ayodhyā*)** in its [Historic City of Ayutthaya](https://whc.unesco.org/en/list/576) account.
- UNESCO's Angkor evaluation derives *Angkor* through Khmer *nokor* from Sanskrit **नगर (*nagara*)**, "city; capital"; see the [Angkor advisory-body evaluation](https://whc.unesco.org/archive/advisory_body_evaluation/668.pdf).

Johannes Bronkhorst, ["The Spread of Sanskrit in Southeast Asia"](https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/early-interactions-between-south-and-southeast-asia/spread-of-sanskrit-in-southeast-asia/1BD7B8481D9B9AF7D1661A8D51C9EF49), in *Early Interactions between South and Southeast Asia* (ISEAS, 2011), pp. 263–276, emphasizes the role of intellectuals and religious professionals and cautions against treating Sanskrit as an everyday regional lingua franca. That distinction supports the chapter's carrier model: specialists and receiving communities transmitted the radiance, while local languages remained the languages of ordinary life.
